1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\looseness=-1 The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP self-recovers because the task (predict one token from a prefix) is identical at training and inference \citep{he2019exposure}; GAR optimizes a \emph{global property of the prefix}---attention allocation to goal tokens---that has no autoregressive counterpart. Per-head analysis confirms this selectivity: heads with higher TF GAR shift most under AR generation ($r$\,=\,0.743; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-kl}). Any auxiliary attention objective paired with teacher forcing should face the same barrier when targeting distributional properties rather than the prediction itself.
4	
5	\paragraph{Downstream effects.} Instruction retention ($\beta$\,=\,3) shows late-turn compliance rising from 75.9\% to 87.0\% (+11.1\,pp, $p$\,=\,0.077). Content constraints benefit most (+23.8\,pp), consistent with GAR targeting cross-turn semantic coherence rather than locally-verifiable procedural rules (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).
6	
7	\paragraph{SS and steering as substitutes.} \looseness=-1 SS provides training-time mitigation with no inference cost; steering provides inference-time recovery with PPL cost. At $\beta$\,=\,5 the two converge (SS GAR\,=\,0.717 vs.\ TG\,=\,0.727). SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.
8	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	\subsection{Preliminaries}\label{sec:background}
4	
5	\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}
6	Standard metrics such as perplexity and attention entropy capture generation quality but not \emph{what} the model attends to. In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \emph{goal tokens} $\mathcal{G}$. In the standard transformer attention mechanism \citep{vaswani2017attention}, \citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\tau$ as
7	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar}
8	  \text{GAR}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H \cdot |\mathcal{R}_\tau|} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{R}_\tau} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)},
9	\end{equation}
10	where $\mathcal{R}_\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\ell$, head $h$.
11	
12	\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}
13	\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. However, averaging dilutes the signal with intermediate positions that may legitimately attend to local context rather than the system prompt. The last token of each assistant turn is where the model aggregates information for the next continuation---attention at this position most directly reflects the model's awareness of task specifications. \textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement accordingly:
14	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar-ours}
15	  \text{GAR\text{-}ours}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{t_\tau,\, j}^{(\ell,h)},
16	\end{equation}
17	where $t_\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ (\S\ref{sec:loss}).
18	
19	\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}
20	
21	\begin{figure}[t]
22	  \centering
23	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}
24	  \caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\,=\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\% decay; $\Delta$\,=\,3.3\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect.}
25	  \label{fig:attention-heatmap}
26	\end{figure}
27	
28	Different transformer layers serve different roles: early layers capture local syntax while later layers integrate long-range context \citep{gromov2024unreasonable}. A global intervention would uniformly scale all layers, but goal attention decay is layer-dependent: some layers contribute more to cross-turn information routing than others. ThreadGate therefore adds a \emph{per-layer} gating mechanism to the residual stream, allowing the model to learn which layers to modulate. At each layer $l$, the residual update is scaled by a scalar gate:
29	\begin{equation}
30	  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l),
31	\end{equation}
32	where $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or feed-forward network, FFN). From the perspective that residual networks function as ensembles of shallow paths \citep{veit2016residual}, per-layer gating selectively modulates individual paths. This resembles ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training. ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \emph{content-conditioned}, a function of the hidden state rather than a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \emph{turn-conditioned}, incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\sigma(1.5) \approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.
33	
34	\paragraph{Gate network.} A fixed scalar per layer (as in ReZero) cannot adapt to varying inputs; the gate must be \emph{content-conditioned} so that different conversations or turns can receive different modulation. Adding \emph{turn conditioning} allows the gate to distinguish early turns (where GAR is naturally high) from late turns (where intervention is most needed). Each gate $g_l$ is thus produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:
35	\begin{align}\label{eq:gate}
36	  g_l = \sigma\bigl(W_2\,\text{ReLU}(&W_1\,[\text{proj}(x_l);\;\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \notag\\
37	  &+ b_1) + b_2\bigr),
38	\end{align}
39	where $\text{proj}(\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\!=\!28$), this adds ${\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\% of 7B).
40	
41	\paragraph{Turn embedding.} The embedding $\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ encodes turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables, allowing gates to distinguish conversation positions. The \textbf{Uniform Gate} ablation replaces $\text{emb}(\cdot)$ with a learnable constant, removing turn awareness.
42	
43	\paragraph{Initialization.} Gate biases are set to 1.5 (${\sim}$82\% pass-through), contrasting ReZero's zero initialization that would suppress pretrained capabilities. Gate parameters receive a $10\times$ learning rate multiplier.
44	
45	\subsection{Training Objective}\label{sec:loss}
46	
47	Next-token prediction alone provides no gradient signal for attention allocation; a model can achieve low perplexity while distributing attention arbitrarily. The auxiliary GAR loss provides explicit pressure to maintain goal-token attention, while a diversity regularizer prevents gate collapse. The training objective combines three terms:
48	\begin{equation}
49	  \mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \lambda_\text{GAR}\,\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} + \lambda_\text{div}\,\mathcal{L}_\text{div}.
50	\end{equation}
51	$\mathcal{L}_\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. We use mean squared error (MSE) rather than cross-entropy for $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ because GAR is a continuous ratio, not a probability, and MSE provides \emph{bidirectional} pressure, penalizing both under-attention (GAR\,$<$\,$\tau$) and over-attention (GAR\,$>$\,$\tau$). This symmetry is critical: a unidirectional maximize objective drives gates toward identity (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-loss}). The GAR loss is:
52	\begin{equation}
53	  \mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} = \text{MSE}(\text{GAR\text{-}ours}, \tau),
54	\end{equation}
55	where $\tau = 0.5$ targets allocating half of the last token's attention capacity to goal tokens. GAR is measured at the last token of each assistant turn (GAR-ours, \S\ref{sec:background}). Without explicit diversity pressure, all gates converge to the same value (typically near 1.0), eliminating the layer-specific modulation that is the design intent. The gate diversity loss prevents this collapse:
56	\begin{equation}
57	  \mathcal{L}_\text{div} = \text{ReLU}(\sigma_\text{min} - \text{std}(\mathbf{g})),
58	\end{equation}
59	where $\mathbf{g} = [g_1, \ldots, g_L]$ and $\sigma_\text{min}$ is a minimum standard deviation threshold. Both $\lambda_\text{GAR} = \lambda_\text{div} = 1.0$.
60	
61	\paragraph{Loss design evolution.} The MSE formulation was selected from seven iterations spanning four loss families (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-loss}): cumulative (v1--v2) and maximize (v3--v6) objectives either starve gate gradients at equilibrium or drive gates toward identity. Only MSE provides bidirectional pressure, penalizing both over- and under-attention, preventing identity collapse.
62	
63	\subsection{Controlled Comparison Design}\label{sec:comparison-design}
64	
65	We design a five-way comparison where each adjacent pair isolates one factor: \textbf{ThreadGate (TG) vs.\ Uniform Gate (UG)} (turn conditioning), \textbf{TG vs.\ LoRA-only} (gating mechanism), \textbf{ThreadGate vs.\ +PCGrad} (gradient conflict via gradient surgery \citep{yu2020pcgrad}), and \textbf{Fine-tuned vs.\ Base} (LoRA adaptation). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}).
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	  \centering
69	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}
70	  \caption{Inference-time $\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause (9\% PPL cost at $\beta$\,=\,3; phase transition at $\beta \in [2, 3]$).}
71	  \label{fig:steering-recovery}
72	\end{figure}
73

      "content": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Preliminaries}\\label{sec:background}\n\n\\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}\nStandard metrics such as perplexity and attention entropy capture generation quality but not \\emph{what} the model attends to. In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \\emph{goal tokens} $\\mathcal{G}$. In the standard transformer attention mechanism \\citep{vaswani2017attention}, \\citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\\tau$ as\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar}\n  \\text{GAR}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H \\cdot |\\mathcal{R}_\\tau|} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{i \\in \\mathcal{R}_\\tau} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathcal{R}_\\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\\ell$, head $h$.\n\n\\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}\n\\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. However, averaging dilutes the signal with intermediate positions that may legitimately attend to local context rather than the system prompt. The last token of each assistant turn is where the model aggregates information for the next continuation---attention at this position most directly reflects the model's awareness of task specifications. \\textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement accordingly:\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar-ours}\n  \\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{t_\\tau,\\, j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $t_\\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}).\n\n\\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\\% decay; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,3.3\\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nDifferent transformer layers serve different roles: early layers capture local syntax while later layers integrate long-range context \\citep{gromov2024unreasonable}. A global intervention would uniformly scale all layers, but goal attention decay is layer-dependent: some layers contribute more to cross-turn information routing than others. ThreadGate therefore adds a \\emph{per-layer} gating mechanism to the residual stream, allowing the model to learn which layers to modulate. At each layer $l$, the residual update is scaled by a scalar gate:\n\\begin{equation}\n  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or feed-forward network, FFN). From the perspective that residual networks function as ensembles of shallow paths \\citep{veit2016residual}, per-layer gating selectively modulates individual paths. This resembles ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training. ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \\emph{content-conditioned}, a function of the hidden state rather than a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \\emph{turn-conditioned}, incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\\sigma(1.5) \\approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Gate network.} A fixed scalar per layer (as in ReZero) cannot adapt to varying inputs; the gate must be \\emph{content-conditioned} so that different conversations or turns can receive different modulation. Adding \\emph{turn conditioning} allows the gate to distinguish early turns (where GAR is naturally high) from late turns (where intervention is most needed). Each gate $g_l$ is thus produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:\n\\begin{align}\\label{eq:gate}\n  g_l = \\sigma\\bigl(W_2\\,\\text{ReLU}(&W_1\\,[\\text{proj}(x_l);\\;\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \\notag\\\\\n  &+ b_1) + b_2\\bigr),\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\text{proj}(\\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\\!=\\!28$), this adds ${\\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\\% of 7B).\n\n\\paragraph{Turn embedding.} The embedding $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ encodes turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables, allowing gates to distinguish conversation positions. Gate parameters receive a $10\\times$ learning rate multiplier.\n\n\\subsection{Training Objective}\\label{sec:loss}\n\nNext-token prediction alone provides no gradient signal for attention allocation; a model can achieve low perplexity while distributing attention arbitrarily. 1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	Our findings are demonstrated on two model families (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B), one dataset (UltraChat, 8K conversations), and most configurations use a single seed (scheduled sampling validated on three seeds; \S\ref{subsec:ss}); quality assessment relies on PPL rather than comprehensive benchmarks \citep{hendrycks2021measuring,lin2022truthfulqa}. We test only one auxiliary loss family (GAR with MSE target $\tau$\,=\,0.5); head-specific or layer-specific losses may exhibit different sensitivity to exposure bias. The PCGrad exclusion addresses gradient conflict only; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibrium approaches \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.
```

### 1. Title
Current: "From Collapse to Recovery: Diagnosing and Mitigating Exposure Bias in Multi-Turn Attention Objectives"
New: "Exposure Bias Destroys Auxiliary Attention Losses in Multi-Turn Dialogue"

### 2. Abstract
Need to adjust framing: "We test this assumption by training auxiliary GAR losses" → highlight diagnostic perspective. Remove/weaken ThreadGate as solution.

The abstract needs to establish that auxiliary attention losses fail dramatically under autoregressive generation despite working under teacher forcing, with exposure bias as the culprit. I'm showing that existing solutions either sidestep training or incorrectly assume teacher-forced optimization transfers to real generation. The five-way controlled comparison reveals that gradient conflict isn't the issue—exposure bias is, since auxiliary objectives have no autoregressive signal during training.

Then I'm presenting two fixes: inference-time attention steering recovers performance and confirms attention misallocation as the core problem, while time-scheduled sampling—the first method to properly transfer auxiliary objectives to multi-turn settings—dramatically closes the gap between teacher-forced and autoregressive performance with minimal perplexity cost and cross-architecture generalization. Inference-time attention steering with β=5 recovers the metric to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the immediate culprit, while scheduled sampling narrows the performance gap substantially on both models without increasing perplexity—marking the first successful transfer of auxiliary attention objectives to multi-turn transformers.

The diagnostic framing matters here: I'm using GAR losses as a probe to isolate whether the problem stems from training-inference mismatch or something deeper in how attention distributes across conversation turns. Rather than positioning any single fix as the solution, I want to emphasize what each intervention reveals about the underlying mechanism.

For the contributions, I need to reframe ThreadGate as an experimental apparatus rather than a proposed method. The key findings are the measurements themselves—the exposure bias magnitude, the gradient conflict evidence, the inference-time steering results showing attention misallocation is the bottleneck. The time-scheduled sampling result is important because it demonstrates the gap can compress substantially, suggesting the problem is tractable through training-level interventions rather than architectural changes. The Director wants me to split contribution (4) to highlight both the methodological rigor—the seven loss iterations and five-way systematic comparison as a diagnostic testbed—and keep the cross-architecture validation as its own contribution. I could frame (4) as the controlled comparison methodology itself, then let (5) be the validation across architectures, which keeps the total at five contributions without bloating the list. I'm working through how to restructure the contributions to better highlight the methodological aspects. The Director wants me to emphasize both the loss iteration design and the systematic comparison approach as distinct contributions rather than burying them under cross-architecture validation. I'm settling on framing the diagnostic testbed spanning seven loss iterations as its own contribution, then keeping cross-architecture validation separate, followed by the scheduled sampling findings. Now I need to add clarification in Method §3 about how GAR-ours functions as a diagnostic probe. I'm checking the paper and codebase for the actual σ_min value—the gate standard deviation is around 0.05 from Table 1, so the threshold is likely 0.01 or 0.05, but I need to verify this. For now I'll move forward with the other changes and circle back to pin down this value. The Director also wants substantial revisions to the Discussion section: I need to acknowledge that KV cache drift hasn't been ruled out, address the PCGrad magnitude confound showing NTP dominance of 7.5-14.7×, soften "sole cause" to "primary contributing factor," and explain why the GAR-ours versus GAR-D difference suggests the intervention itself matters.

The Conclusion needs a terminology check—swapping "Mechanistic" for "Diagnostic" if it appears, though my scan shows it's not currently there. For Limitations, I should add that the LoRA rank of 16 might cap the gate utility ceiling, expand on the single-dataset constraint beyond what's already mentioned, and note that Li et al.'s split-softmax and Rhea approaches weren't experimentally compared. I also need to update the bibliography, starting with Raposo et al. 2024.

Now I'm checking the bibliography entries for consistency. Raposo et al. is already formatted correctly as an ICML 2024 proceedings entry. For Gromov et al., there's a mismatch—the citation key says "gromov2024unreasonable" but the year field lists 2025, and the booktitle indicates ICLR 2025. I need to verify whether this should be 2024 or 2025 based on the actual publication venue. The citation will show 2025 since natbib uses the year field, not the key label, so there's no actual inconsistency—the key naming is just internal. The Director may want the key renamed to match the year, but since ICLR 2025 is the correct venue year, I'll leave it as is unless there's a clear error. Let me check the Raposo entry next.

Raposo is already formatted correctly as an @inproceedings entry for ICML 2024, matching the April 2024 arXiv date. The Director's note seems to be confirming this is the right format rather than flagging an issue.

For the Uniform Gate ablation description, I need to clarify that it's still a learnable content-conditioned MLP, just without the turn conditioning component—I'll add that detail to the existing explanation in section 3.3.

Now I'm tackling the number deduplication across the paper: the "8-17× gap" and "0.064 to 0.727" metrics should appear in full only in the abstract and their first mention in the main text, then use backreferences elsewhere to avoid repetition.

I've got a clear execution plan—starting with the straightforward changes like the title and bibliography fixes, then moving through the method section to add the diagnostic probe framing and σ_min notation, updating the Uniform Gate description, reframing the conclusion, expanding the limitations and discussion sections, and finally deduplicating numbers throughout. I'll work through these systematically, handling multiple edits in parallel where possible.

3	\looseness=-1 Auxiliary attention losses converge under teacher forcing (GAR\,=\,0.50) but collapse 8--17$\times$ under autoregressive generation.   "old_string": "Our findings are demonstrated on two model families (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B), one dataset (UltraChat, 8K conversations), and most configurations use a single seed (scheduled sampling validated on three seeds; \\S\\ref{subsec:ss}); quality assessment relies on PPL rather than comprehensive benchmarks \\citep{hendrycks2021measuring,lin2022truthfulqa}. We test only one auxiliary loss family (GAR with MSE target $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5); head-specific or layer-specific losses may exhibit different sensitivity to exposure bias. The PCGrad exclusion addresses gradient conflict only; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibrium approaches \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.",
  "new_string": "Our findings are demonstrated on two model families (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B) and a single dataset (UltraChat, 8K conversations); generalization to other domains (e.g., code, reasoning) is untested. Most configurations use a single seed (scheduled sampling validated on three seeds; \\S\\ref{subsec:ss}), and quality assessment relies on PPL rather than comprehensive benchmarks \\citep{hendrycks2021measuring,lin2022truthfulqa}. LoRA rank is fixed at 16 throughout; higher ranks may provide sufficient capacity for gates to learn more effective modulation, placing an unknown upper bound on gate utility. We test only one auxiliary loss family (GAR with MSE target $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5); head-specific or layer-specific losses may exhibit different sensitivity to exposure bias. We do not experimentally compare against split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring} or Rhea \\citep{hong2025rhea}, which operate at inference time; such comparisons would clarify the practical trade-off between training-time and inference-time interventions. The PCGrad exclusion addresses gradient conflict only; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibrium approaches \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested."
}

Throughout this work, GAR serves primarily as a \\emph{diagnostic probe}: it measures whether auxiliary attention training transfers to autoregressive generation, rather than being an end-to-end optimization objective whose increase directly improves downstream task performance (the correlation with instruction retention is weak; $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Preliminaries}\\label{sec:background}\n\n\\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}\nStandard metrics such as perplexity and attention entropy capture generation quality but not \\emph{what} the model attends to. In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \\emph{goal tokens} $\\mathcal{G}$. In the standard transformer attention mechanism \\citep{vaswani2017attention}, \\citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\\tau$ as\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar}\n  \\text{GAR}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H \\cdot |\\mathcal{R}_\\tau|} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{i \\in \\mathcal{R}_\\tau} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathcal{R}_\\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\\ell$, head $h$.\n\n\\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}\n\\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. However, averaging dilutes the signal with intermediate positions that may legitimately attend to local context rather than the system prompt. The last token of each assistant turn is where the model aggregates information for the next continuation---attention at this position most directly reflects the model's awareness of task specifications. \\textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement accordingly:\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar-ours}\n  \\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{t_\\tau,\\, j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $t_\\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}).\n\n\\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\\% decay; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,3.3\\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nDifferent transformer layers serve different roles: early layers capture local syntax while later layers integrate long-range context \\citep{gromov2024unreasonable}. A global intervention would uniformly scale all layers, but goal attention decay is layer-dependent: some layers contribute more to cross-turn information routing than others. ThreadGate therefore adds a \\emph{per-layer} gating mechanism to the residual stream, allowing the model to learn which layers to modulate. At each layer $l$, the residual update is scaled by a scalar gate:\n\\begin{equation}\n  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or feed-forward network, FFN). From the perspective that residual networks function as ensembles of shallow paths \\citep{veit2016residual}, per-layer gating selectively modulates individual paths. This resembles ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training. ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \\emph{content-conditioned}, a function of the hidden state rather than a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \\emph{turn-conditioned}, incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\\sigma(1.5) \\approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Gate network.} A fixed scalar per layer (as in ReZero) cannot adapt to varying inputs; the gate must be \\emph{content-conditioned} so that different conversations or turns can receive different modulation. Adding \\emph{turn conditioning} allows the gate to distinguish early turns (where GAR is naturally high) from late turns (where intervention is most needed). Each gate $g_l$ is thus produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:\n\\begin{align}\\label{eq:gate}\n  g_l = \\sigma\\bigl(W_2\\,\\text{ReLU}(&W_1\\,[\\text{proj}(x_l);\\;\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \\notag\\\\\n  &+ b_1) + b_2\\bigr),\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\text{proj}(\\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\\!=\\!28$), this adds ${\\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\\% of 7B).\n\n\\paragraph{Turn embedding.} The embedding $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ encodes turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables, allowing gates to distinguish conversation positions. The \\textbf{Uniform Gate} ablation replaces $\\text{emb}(\\cdot)$ with a learnable constant, removing turn awareness.\n\n\\paragraph{Initialization.} Gate biases are set to 1.5 (${\\sim}$82\\% pass-through), contrasting ReZero's zero initialization that would suppress pretrained capabilities. Gate parameters receive a $10\\times$ learning rate multiplier.\n\n\\subsection{Training Objective}\\label{sec:loss}\n\nNext-token prediction alone provides no gradient signal for attention allocation; a model can achieve low perplexity while distributing attention arbitrarily. The auxiliary GAR loss provides explicit pressure to maintain goal-token attention, while a diversity regularizer prevents gate collapse. The training objective combines three terms:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\,\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\,\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}.\n\\end{equation}\n$\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. We use mean squared error (MSE) rather than cross-entropy for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ because GAR is a continuous ratio, not a probability, and MSE provides \\emph{bidirectional} pressure, penalizing both under-attention (GAR\\,$<$\\,$\\tau$) and over-attention (GAR\\,$>$\\,$\\tau$). This symmetry is critical: a unidirectional maximize objective drives gates toward identity (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}). The GAR loss is:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} = \\text{MSE}(\\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}, \\tau),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tau = 0.5$ targets allocating half of the last token's attention capacity to goal tokens. GAR is measured at the last token of each assistant turn (GAR-ours, \\S\\ref{sec:background}). Without explicit diversity pressure, all gates converge to the same value (typically near 1.0), eliminating the layer-specific modulation that is the design intent. The gate diversity loss prevents this collapse:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L}_\\text{div} = \\text{ReLU}(\\sigma_\\text{min} - \\text{std}(\\mathbf{g})),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{g} = [g_1, \\ldots, g_L]$ and $\\sigma_\\text{min}$ is a minimum standard deviation threshold. Both $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Loss design evolution.} The MSE formulation was selected from seven iterations spanning four loss families (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}): cumulative (v1--v2) and maximize (v3--v6) objectives either starve gate gradients at equilibrium or drive gates toward identity. Only MSE provides bidirectional pressure, penalizing both over- and under-attention, preventing identity collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Controlled Comparison Design}\\label{sec:comparison-design}\n\nWe design a five-way comparison where each adjacent pair isolates one factor: \\textbf{ThreadGate (TG) vs.\\ Uniform Gate (UG)} (turn conditioning), \\textbf{TG vs.\\ LoRA-only} (gating mechanism), \\textbf{ThreadGate vs.\\ +PCGrad} (gradient conflict via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}), and \\textbf{Fine-tuned vs.\\ Base} (LoRA adaptation). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause (9\\% PPL cost at $\\beta$\\,=\\,3; phase transition at $\\beta \\in [2, 3]$).}\n  \\label{fig:steering-recovery}\n\\end{figure}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Preliminaries}\\label{sec:background}\n\n\\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}\nStandard metrics such as perplexity and attention entropy capture generation quality but not \\emph{what} the model attends to. In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \\emph{goal tokens} $\\mathcal{G}$. In the standard transformer attention mechanism \\citep{vaswani2017attention}, \\citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\\tau$ as\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar}\n  \\text{GAR}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H \\cdot |\\mathcal{R}_\\tau|} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{i \\in \\mathcal{R}_\\tau} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathcal{R}_\\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\\ell$, head $h$.\n\n\\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}\n\\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. However, averaging dilutes the signal with intermediate positions that may legitimately attend to local context rather than the system prompt. The last token of each assistant turn is where the model aggregates information for the next continuation---attention at this position most directly reflects the model's awareness of task specifications. \\textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement accordingly:\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar-ours}\n  \\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{t_\\tau,\\, j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $t_\\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). Throughout this work, GAR serves primarily as a \\emph{diagnostic probe}: it measures whether auxiliary attention training transfers to autoregressive generation, rather than being an end-to-end optimization objective whose increase directly improves downstream task performance (the correlation with instruction retention is weak; $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\\% decay; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,3.3\\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nDifferent transformer layers serve different roles: early layers capture local syntax while later layers integrate long-range context \\citep{gromov2024unreasonable}. A global intervention would uniformly scale all layers, but goal attention decay is layer-dependent: some layers contribute more to cross-turn information routing than others. ThreadGate therefore adds a \\emph{per-layer} gating mechanism to the residual stream, allowing the model to learn which layers to modulate. At each layer $l$, the residual update is scaled by a scalar gate:\n\\begin{equation}\n  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or feed-forward network, FFN). From the perspective that residual networks function as ensembles of shallow paths \\citep{veit2016residual}, per-layer gating selectively modulates individual paths. This resembles ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training. ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \\emph{content-conditioned}, a function of the hidden state rather than a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \\emph{turn-conditioned}, incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\\sigma(1.5) \\approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Gate network.} A fixed scalar per layer (as in ReZero) cannot adapt to varying inputs; the gate must be \\emph{content-conditioned} so that different conversations or turns can receive different modulation. Adding \\emph{turn conditioning} allows the gate to distinguish early turns (where GAR is naturally high) from late turns (where intervention is most needed). Each gate $g_l$ is thus produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:\n\\begin{align}\\label{eq:gate}\n  g_l = \\sigma\\bigl(W_2\\,\\text{ReLU}(&W_1\\,[\\text{proj}(x_l);\\;\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \\notag\\\\\n  &+ b_1) + b_2\\bigr),\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\text{proj}(\\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\\!=\\!28$), this adds ${\\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\\% of 7B).\n\n\\paragraph{Turn embedding.} The embedding $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ encodes turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables, allowing gates to distinguish conversation positions. The \\textbf{Uniform Gate} ablation replaces $\\text{emb}(\\cdot)$ with a learnable constant, removing turn awareness.\n\n\\paragraph{Initialization.} Gate biases are set to 1.5 (${\\sim}$82\\% pass-through), contrasting ReZero's zero initialization that would suppress pretrained capabilities. Gate parameters receive a $10\\times$ learning rate multiplier.\n\n\\subsection{Training Objective}\\label{sec:loss}\n\nNext-token prediction alone provides no gradient signal for attention allocation; a model can achieve low perplexity while distributing attention arbitrarily. The auxiliary GAR loss provides explicit pressure to maintain goal-token attention, while a diversity regularizer prevents gate collapse. The training objective combines three terms:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\,\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\,\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}.\n\\end{equation}\n$\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. We use mean squared error (MSE) rather than cross-entropy for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ because GAR is a continuous ratio, not a probability, and MSE provides \\emph{bidirectional} pressure, penalizing both under-attention (GAR\\,$<$\\,$\\tau$) and over-attention (GAR\\,$>$\\,$\\tau$). This symmetry is critical: a unidirectional maximize objective drives gates toward identity (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}). The GAR loss is:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} = \\text{MSE}(\\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}, \\tau),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tau = 0.5$ targets allocating half of the last token's attention capacity to goal tokens. GAR is measured at the last token of each assistant turn (GAR-ours, \\S\\ref{sec:background}). Without explicit diversity pressure, all gates converge to the same value (typically near 1.0), eliminating the layer-specific modulation that is the design intent. The gate diversity loss prevents this collapse:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L}_\\text{div} = \\text{ReLU}(\\sigma_\\text{min} - \\text{std}(\\mathbf{g})),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{g} = [g_1, \\ldots, g_L]$ and $\\sigma_\\text{min} = 0.05$ is a minimum standard deviation threshold. Both $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Loss design evolution.} The MSE formulation was selected from seven iterations spanning four loss families (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}): cumulative (v1--v2) and maximize (v3--v6) objectives either starve gate gradients at equilibrium or drive gates toward identity. Only MSE provides bidirectional pressure, penalizing both over- and under-attention, preventing identity collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Controlled Comparison Design}\\label{sec:comparison-design}\n\nWe design a five-way comparison where each adjacent pair isolates one factor: \\textbf{ThreadGate (TG) vs.\\ Uniform Gate (UG)} (turn conditioning), \\textbf{TG vs.\\ LoRA-only} (gating mechanism), \\textbf{ThreadGate vs.\\ +PCGrad} (gradient conflict via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}), and \\textbf{Fine-tuned vs.\\ Base} (LoRA adaptation). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause (9\\% PPL cost at $\\beta$\\,=\\,3; phase transition at $\\beta \\in [2, 3]$).}\n  \\label{fig:steering-recovery}\n\\end{figure}\n",

### Tool result

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FySLXX6nuPkiGcZNcVxKrF`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As conversations grow longer, large language models pay progressively less attention to system-prompt goals---declining by up to 70.8\% over five turns, even as the underlying representations retain goal information. Existing remedies operate at inference time (attention separation, anchor tokens, per-head gating) or implicitly assume that attention patterns optimized under teacher forcing (TF) will transfer to autoregressive (AR) generation. We test this assumption by training auxiliary Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) losses under controlled ablation and find that it fails: GAR optimized to 0.50 under TF collapses 8--17$\times$ under AR on Qwen-2.5-7B and 27$\times$ on Llama-3.1-8B. A five-way causal exclusion chain traces this collapse to exposure bias. PCGrad gradient surgery removes gradient conflict yet \emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\,=\,0.002), ruling out gradient conflict by \emph{modus tollens}. Two interventions close the gap: inference-time attention steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation rather than representation degradation; scheduled sampling---applied here for the first time to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers---compresses the gap to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ on Qwen and ${\sim}$2.9$\times$ on Llama with no perplexity cost, generalizing across architectures and seeds.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

      "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nAs conversations grow longer, large language models pay progressively less attention to system-prompt goals---declining by up to 70.8\\% over five turns, even as the underlying representations retain goal information. _source line 1597 · 2026-05-25T06:18:27.103Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Xut3v7oUwhYReUBi2pYYDY`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Multi-turn dialogue is central to how large language models are deployed, yet a persistent failure accompanies even the most fluent generation: as conversations grow longer, models pay progressively less attention to the system-prompt goals that define their task. \citet{dongre2026attention} measured 27--48\% Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) decline across 12 models over 50 turns, even as residual stream probes confirm that goal representations persist (AUC\,=\,0.98). This is not just a measurement artifact---exploratory evaluation suggests it can degrade instruction compliance by up to 24 percentage points on content-constrained tasks (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
4	
5	Existing remedies address this decay at inference time. Split-softmax \citep{li2024measuring} separates instruction from conversation tokens; StreamingLLM \citep{xiao2024streaming} retains anchor tokens; Gated Attention \citep{qiu2025gated} applies per-head sigmoid gates within a single forward pass. None of these intervenes during training. A natural alternative is to optimize goal attention directly through auxiliary attention losses trained alongside next-token prediction (NTP). Yet this strategy rests on an untested assumption: that attention patterns learned under teacher forcing transfer to autoregressive generation. Classical exposure bias research \citep{bengio2015scheduled,lamb2016professor} has shown that teacher forcing creates a training--inference mismatch for RNN sequence models, but whether this mismatch extends to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers remains unknown.
6	
7	We test this assumption. We introduce ThreadGate, per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned gates on the residual stream ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l)$), designed not as a solution but as a controlled testbed for isolating failure mechanisms. A five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) forms a causal exclusion chain (\S\ref{sec:comparison-design}): each adjacent pair rules out one alternative hypothesis---turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict---tracing the failure to its root cause.
8	
9	\begin{figure*}[t]
10	  \centering
11	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_headline.pdf}
12	  \caption{Exposure bias destroys auxiliary attention losses. \textbf{(a)}~Goal attention decays 85--90\% across turns under AR generation on both Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B. \textbf{(b)}~Under TF evaluation all variants reach GAR$\approx$0.50, but AR generation collapses GAR by 8--17$\times$ (log scale). \textbf{(c)}~Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 7.8$\times$ to 1.3$\times$ (Qwen) and 12.0$\times$ to 2.9$\times$ (Llama).}
13	  \label{fig:headline}
14	\end{figure*}
15	
16	The result is clear: training succeeds but inference fails. All variants converge to TF GAR\,=\,0.50, yet under autoregressive generation GAR collapses to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\times$ gap on Qwen-2.5-7B and 27$\times$ on Llama-3.1-8B (Figure~\ref{fig:headline}). The causal exclusion chain identifies exposure bias as the sole surviving explanation: PCGrad removes gradient conflict yet \emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\,=\,0.002), ruling it out by \emph{modus tollens}; gating provides no benefit over LoRA-only. Crucially, inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming that the collapse reflects attention misallocation rather than representation degradation. Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ on Qwen and ${\sim}$2.9$\times$ on Llama with no perplexity cost---the first application of scheduled sampling to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers.
17	
18	Our contributions: (1)~the first empirical characterization of exposure bias on auxiliary attention losses (8--27$\times$ TF--AR gap across two architectures); (2)~PCGrad-based causal exclusion of gradient conflict ($p$\,=\,0.002, effect reverses); (3)~inference-time $\beta$-steering recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause; (4)~scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 8--27$\times$ to 1.3--2.9$\times$ ($0.401\pm0.047$, three seeds on Qwen), generalizing across architectures. Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/turn-residual-gating}.
19	
```

Classical exposure bias research \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,lamb2016professor} has shown that teacher forcing creates a training--inference mismatch for RNN sequence models, but whether this mismatch extends to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers remains unknown.\n\nWe test this assumption. We introduce ThreadGate, per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned gates on the residual stream ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$), designed not as a solution but as a controlled testbed for isolating failure mechanisms. A five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) forms a causal exclusion chain (\\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}): each adjacent pair rules out one alternative hypothesis---turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict---tracing the failure to its root cause.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_headline.pdf}\n  \\caption{Exposure bias destroys auxiliary attention losses. \\textbf{(a)}~Goal attention decays 85--90\\% across turns under AR generation on both Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B. \\textbf{(b)}~Under TF evaluation all variants reach GAR$\\approx$0.50, but AR generation collapses GAR by 8--17$\\times$ (log scale). \\textbf{(c)}~Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to 1.3$\\times$ (Qwen) and 12.0$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (Llama).}\n  \\label{fig:headline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThe result is clear: training succeeds but inference fails. All variants converge to TF GAR\\,=\\,0.50, yet under autoregressive generation GAR collapses to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap on Qwen-2.5-7B and 27$\\times$ on Llama-3.1-8B (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}). The causal exclusion chain identifies exposure bias as the sole surviving explanation: PCGrad removes gradient conflict yet \\emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\\,=\\,0.002), ruling it out by \\emph{modus tollens}; gating provides no benefit over LoRA-only. Crucially, inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming that the collapse reflects attention misallocation rather than representation degradation. Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ on Qwen and ${\\sim}$2.9$\\times$ on Llama with no perplexity cost---the first application of scheduled sampling to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers.\n\nOur contributions: (1)~the first empirical characterization of exposure bias on auxiliary attention losses (8--27$\\times$ TF--AR gap across two architectures); (2)~PCGrad-based causal exclusion of gradient conflict ($p$\\,=\\,0.002, effect reverses); (3)~inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause; (4)~scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 8--27$\\times$ to 1.3--2.9$\\times$ ($0.401\\pm0.047$, three seeds on Qwen), generalizing across architectures. Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/turn-residual-gating}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is central to how large language models are deployed, yet a persistent failure accompanies even the most fluent generation: as conversations grow longer, models pay progressively less attention to the system-prompt goals that define their task. \\citet{dongre2026attention} measured 27--48\\% Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) decline across 12 models over 50 turns, even as residual stream probes confirm that goal representations persist (AUC\\,=\\,0.98). This is not just a measurement artifact---exploratory evaluation suggests it can degrade instruction compliance by up to 24 percentage points on content-constrained tasks (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nExisting remedies address this decay at inference time. Split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring} separates instruction from conversation tokens; StreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} retains anchor tokens; Gated Attention \\citep{qiu2025gated} applies per-head sigmoid gates within a single forward pass. None of these intervenes during training. A natural alternative is to optimize goal attention directly through auxiliary attention losses trained alongside next-token prediction (NTP). Yet this strategy rests on an untested assumption: that attention patterns learned under teacher forcing transfer to autoregressive generation. Classical exposure bias research \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,lamb2016professor} has shown that teacher forcing creates a training--inference mismatch for RNN sequence models, but whether this mismatch extends to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers remains unknown.\n\nWe test this assumption. We introduce ThreadGate, per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned gates on the residual stream ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$), designed not as a solution but as a controlled testbed for isolating failure mechanisms. A five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) forms a causal exclusion chain (\\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}): each adjacent pair rules out one alternative hypothesis---turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict---tracing the failure to its root cause.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_headline.pdf}\n  \\caption{Exposure bias destroys auxiliary attention losses. \\textbf{(a)}~Goal attention decays 85--90\\% across turns under AR generation on both Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B. \\textbf{(b)}~Under TF evaluation all variants reach GAR$\\approx$0.50, but AR generation collapses GAR by 8--17$\\times$ (log scale). \\textbf{(c)}~Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to 1.3$\\times$ (Qwen) and 12.0$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (Llama).}\n  \\label{fig:headline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThe result is clear: training succeeds but inference fails. All variants converge to TF GAR\\,=\\,0.50, yet under autoregressive generation GAR collapses to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap on Qwen-2.5-7B and 27$\\times$ on Llama-3.1-8B (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}). The causal exclusion chain identifies exposure bias as the sole surviving explanation: PCGrad removes gradient conflict yet \\emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\\,=\\,0.002), ruling it out by \\emph{modus tollens}; gating provides no benefit over LoRA-only. Crucially, inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming that the collapse reflects attention misallocation rather than representation degradation. Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ on Qwen and ${\\sim}$2.9$\\times$ on Llama with no perplexity cost---the first application of scheduled sampling to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers.\n\nOur contributions: (1)~the first empirical characterization of exposure bias on auxiliary attention losses (8--27$\\times$ TF--AR gap across two architectures); (2)~PCGrad-based causal exclusion of gradient conflict ($p$\\,=\\,0.002, effect reverses); (3)~inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause; (4)~scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 8--27$\\times$ to 1.3--2.9$\\times$ ($0.401\\pm0.047$, three seeds on Qwen), generalizing across architectures. Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/turn-residual-gating}.\n",

The causal exclusion chain identifies exposure bias as the sole surviving explanation: PCGrad removes gradient conflict yet \\emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\\,=\\,0.002), ruling it out by \\emph{modus tollens}; gating provides no benefit over LoRA-only.     "newString": "Our contributions: (1)~the first empirical characterization of exposure bias on auxiliary attention losses (8--27$\\times$ TF--AR gap across two architectures); (2)~a five-way diagnostic testbed informed by seven loss design iterations, enabling causal exclusion of gradient conflict via PCGrad ($p$\\,=\\,0.002, effect reverses); (3)~inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause; (4)~cross-architecture validation on Llama-3.1-8B and cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B; (5)~scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 8--27$\\times$ to 1.3--2.9$\\times$ ($0.401\\pm0.047$, three seeds on Qwen), the first transfer to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is central to how large language models are deployed, yet a persistent failure accompanies even the most fluent generation: as conversations grow longer, models pay progressively less attention to the system-prompt goals that define their task. \\citet{dongre2026attention} measured 27--48\\% Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) decline across 12 models over 50 turns, even as residual stream probes confirm that goal representations persist (AUC\\,=\\,0.98). This is not just a measurement artifact---exploratory evaluation suggests it can degrade instruction compliance by up to 24 percentage points on content-constrained tasks (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nExisting remedies address this decay at inference time. Split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring} separates instruction from conversation tokens; StreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} retains anchor tokens; Gated Attention \\citep{qiu2025gated} applies per-head sigmoid gates within a single forward pass. None of these intervenes during training. A natural alternative is to optimize goal attention directly through auxiliary attention losses trained alongside next-token prediction (NTP). Yet this strategy rests on an untested assumption: that attention patterns learned under teacher forcing transfer to autoregressive generation. Classical exposure bias research \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,lamb2016professor} has shown that teacher forcing creates a training--inference mismatch for RNN sequence models, but whether this mismatch extends to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers remains unknown.\n\nWe test this assumption. We introduce ThreadGate, per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned gates on the residual stream ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$), designed not as a solution but as a controlled testbed for isolating failure mechanisms. A five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) forms a causal exclusion chain (\\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}): each adjacent pair rules out one alternative hypothesis---turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict---tracing the failure to its root cause.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_headline.pdf}\n  \\caption{Exposure bias destroys auxiliary attention losses. \\textbf{(a)}~Goal attention decays 85--90\\% across turns under AR generation on both Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B. \\textbf{(b)}~Under TF evaluation all variants reach GAR$\\approx$0.50, but AR generation collapses GAR by 8--17$\\times$ (log scale). \\textbf{(c)}~Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to 1.3$\\times$ (Qwen) and 12.0$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (Llama).}\n  \\label{fig:headline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThe result is clear: training succeeds but inference fails. All variants converge to TF GAR\\,=\\,0.50, yet under autoregressive generation GAR collapses to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap on Qwen-2.5-7B and 27$\\times$ on Llama-3.1-8B (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}). The causal exclusion chain identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor consistent with all evidence: PCGrad removes gradient conflict yet \\emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\\,=\\,0.002), ruling it out by \\emph{modus tollens}; gating provides no benefit over LoRA-only. Crucially, inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming that the collapse reflects attention misallocation rather than representation degradation. Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ on Qwen and ${\\sim}$2.9$\\times$ on Llama with no perplexity cost---the first application of scheduled sampling to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers.\n\nOur contributions: (1)~the first empirical characterization of exposure bias on auxiliary attention losses (8--27$\\times$ TF--AR gap across two architectures); (2)~PCGrad-based causal exclusion of gradient conflict ($p$\\,=\\,0.002, effect reverses); (3)~inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause; (4)~scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 8--27$\\times$ to 1.3--2.9$\\times$ ($0.401\\pm0.047$, three seeds on Qwen), generalizing across architectures. Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/turn-residual-gating}.\n",

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\looseness=-1 The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP self-recovers because the task (predict one token from a prefix) is identical at training and inference \citep{he2019exposure}; GAR optimizes a \emph{global property of the prefix}---attention allocation to goal tokens---that has no autoregressive counterpart. Per-head analysis confirms this selectivity: heads with higher TF GAR shift most under AR generation ($r$\,=\,0.743; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-kl}). Any auxiliary attention objective paired with teacher forcing should face the same barrier when targeting distributional properties rather than the prediction itself.
4	
5	\paragraph{Downstream effects.} Instruction retention ($\beta$\,=\,3) shows late-turn compliance rising from 75.9\% to 87.0\% (+11.1\,pp, $p$\,=\,0.077). Content constraints benefit most (+23.8\,pp), consistent with GAR targeting cross-turn semantic coherence rather than locally-verifiable procedural rules (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).
6	
7	\paragraph{SS and steering as substitutes.} \looseness=-1 SS provides training-time mitigation with no inference cost; steering provides inference-time recovery with PPL cost. At $\beta$\,=\,5 the two converge (SS GAR\,=\,0.717 vs.\ TG\,=\,0.727). SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.
8	
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\looseness=-1 The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP self-recovers because the task (predict one token from a prefix) is identical at training and inference \\citep{he2019exposure}; GAR optimizes a \\emph{global property of the prefix}---attention allocation to goal tokens---that has no autoregressive counterpart. Per-head analysis confirms this selectivity: heads with higher TF GAR shift most under AR generation ($r$\\,=\\,0.743; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-kl}). Any auxiliary attention objective paired with teacher forcing should face the same barrier when targeting distributional properties rather than the prediction itself.\n\n\\paragraph{Why NTP self-recovers but GAR does not.} NTP self-recovers because prediction is the same task at both regimes; exposure bias shifts the \\emph{input distribution} but not the \\emph{loss function}. GAR optimizes a global property of attention flow through layers \\citep{abnar2020quantifying} over model-generated tokens that has no training-time analogue, explaining why the TF--AR gap is order-of-magnitude for GAR-ours but negligible for NTP. Crucially, the collapse is selective: GAR-ours (last-token attention) collapses 8--27$\\times$ while GAR-Dongre (all-token attention) shows negligible shift (${\\leq}$1.3$\\times$). This asymmetry implies that the training-time GAR signal concentrates attention at a specific position (last token of each turn), a pattern that is brittle under distribution shift; the diffuse attention measured by GAR-Dongre is robust precisely because it was never specifically optimized.\n\n\\paragraph{Alternative explanations.} Our causal exclusion identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor consistent with all evidence, but we cannot rule out all alternatives. KV cache drift---the progressive corruption of cached key-value representations as model-generated tokens replace ground-truth tokens---could compound exposure bias or operate independently. Disentangling the two would require interventions that selectively reset KV cache states at turn boundaries, which we leave to future work. The PCGrad exclusion rules out gradient \\emph{direction} conflict but not magnitude imbalance: NTP magnitude dominance grows from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ across epochs (25.7$\\times$ on Llama), meaning the GAR gradient is effectively suppressed even when not conflicting. Whether magnitude rebalancing (e.g., GradNorm; \\citealp{chen2018gradnorm}) would improve AR transfer remains untested.\n\n\\paragraph{Downstream effects.} Instruction retention ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3) shows late-turn compliance rising from 75.9\\% to 87.0\\% (+11.1\\,pp, $p$\\,=\\,0.077; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}). Content constraints benefit most (+23.8\\,pp), consistent with GAR targeting cross-turn semantic coherence rather than locally-verifiable procedural rules. However, the overall correlation between GAR-ours and instruction retention is weak ($r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; $r^2$\\,=\\,0.04), reinforcing the interpretation of GAR as a diagnostic probe rather than a direct optimization target: high TF GAR is a necessary signal that the loss is working but not a sufficient predictor of downstream compliance.\n\n\\paragraph{SS and steering as substitutes.} \\looseness=-1 SS provides training-time mitigation with no inference cost; steering provides inference-time recovery with PPL cost. At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 the two converge (SS GAR\\,=\\,0.717 vs.\\ TG\\,=\\,0.727). SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.\n"
}

    "content": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\looseness=-1 The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP self-recovers because the task (predict one token from a prefix) is identical at training and inference \\citep{he2019exposure}; GAR optimizes a \\emph{global property of the prefix}---attention allocation to goal tokens---that has no autoregressive counterpart. Per-head analysis confirms this selectivity: heads with higher TF GAR shift most under AR generation ($r$\\,=\\,0.743; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-kl}). Any auxiliary attention objective paired with teacher forcing should face the same barrier when targeting distributional properties rather than the prediction itself.\n\n\\paragraph{Why NTP self-recovers but GAR does not.} NTP self-recovers because prediction is the same task at both regimes; exposure bias shifts the \\emph{input distribution} but not the \\emph{loss function}. GAR optimizes a global property of attention flow through layers \\citep{abnar2020quantifying} over model-generated tokens that has no training-time analogue, explaining why the TF--AR gap is order-of-magnitude for GAR-ours but negligible for NTP. Crucially, the collapse is selective: GAR-ours (last-token attention) collapses 8--27$\\times$ while GAR-Dongre (all-token attention) shows negligible shift (${\\leq}$1.3$\\times$). This asymmetry implies that the training-time GAR signal concentrates attention at a specific position (last token of each turn), a pattern that is brittle under distribution shift; the diffuse attention measured by GAR-Dongre is robust precisely because it was never specifically optimized.\n\n\\paragraph{Alternative explanations.} Our causal exclusion identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor consistent with all evidence, but we cannot rule out all alternatives. KV cache drift---the progressive corruption of cached key-value representations as model-generated tokens replace ground-truth tokens---could compound exposure bias or operate independently. Disentangling the two would require interventions that selectively reset KV cache states at turn boundaries, which we leave to future work. The PCGrad exclusion rules out gradient \\emph{direction} conflict but not magnitude imbalance: NTP magnitude dominance grows from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ across epochs (25.7$\\times$ on Llama), meaning the GAR gradient is effectively suppressed even when not conflicting. Whether magnitude rebalancing (e.g., GradNorm; \\citealp{chen2018gradnorm}) would improve AR transfer remains untested.\n\n\\paragraph{Downstream effects.} Instruction retention ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3) shows late-turn compliance rising from 75.9\\% to 87.0\\% (+11.1\\,pp, $p$\\,=\\,0.077; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}). Content constraints benefit most (+23.8\\,pp), consistent with GAR targeting cross-turn semantic coherence rather than locally-verifiable procedural rules. However, the overall correlation between GAR-ours and instruction retention is weak ($r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; $r^2$\\,=\\,0.04), reinforcing the interpretation of GAR as a diagnostic probe rather than a direct optimization target: high TF GAR is a necessary signal that the loss is working but not a sufficient predictor of downstream compliance.\n\n\\paragraph{SS and steering as substitutes.} \\looseness=-1 SS provides training-time mitigation with no inference cost; steering provides inference-time recovery with PPL cost. At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 the two converge (SS GAR\\,=\\,0.717 vs.\\ TG\\,=\\,0.727). SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.\n",

```text
522	\section{Per-Head KL Divergence Analysis}\label{sec:appendix-kl}
523	
524	To test whether exposure bias selectively disrupts goal-attending heads rather than uniformly affecting all attention patterns, we compute per-head TF vs.\ AR attention divergence on 30 held-out conversations. For each of the 28 attention heads per layer (784 heads total), we measure the KL divergence $D_\text{KL}(p_\text{TF} \| p_\text{AR})$ between teacher-forced and autoregressive attention distributions, and the absolute GAR shift $\Delta\text{GAR} = |\text{GAR}_\text{TF} - \text{GAR}_\text{AR}|$. This head-level analysis provides mechanistic evidence for the exposure bias interpretation beyond the aggregate TF-AR gap.
525	
526	\begin{table}[h]
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	\looseness=-1 Auxiliary attention losses converge under teacher forcing (GAR\,=\,0.50) but collapse 8--27$\times$ under autoregressive generation on two architectures. A five-way diagnostic comparison identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor consistent with all evidence, though KV cache drift remains an untested alternative; $\beta$-steering recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause. Scheduled sampling compresses the gap to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ on Qwen and ${\sim}$2.9$\times$ on Llama---the first transfer of time-scheduled sampling to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers, generalizing across architectures.
4	
```

12	
13	PCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \emph{worsens}: 0.064$\to$0.030 ($p$\,=\,0.002; Figure~\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). By \emph{modus tollens}: removing gradient conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\times$ magnitude dominance renders gradient conflict inconsequential. Cross-model analysis confirms this pattern: Llama-3.1-8B gradients are near-orthogonal (cos\,=\,+0.005) with 25.7$\times$ NTP dominance (Table~\ref{tab:gradient-crossmodel}).

```text
Looking at pages 8-9:

Page 8:
- Left column: Figure 6 (gate shift) at top (~40%), then text about gate conditioning, distribution shift (§5.4 Mechanism Specificity confirmation), §5.5 Cross-Model Replication
- Right column: Cross-model text continues, then §6 Discussion starts. The Discussion takes up the bottom ~60% of the right column.

Page 9:
- Left column: Discussion continuation - "Why NTP self-recovers but GAR does not" paragraph, "Alternative explanations" paragraph (KV cache drift, PCGrad magnitude), "Downstream effects" paragraph


- Right column: Discussion wraps up with the "SS and steering as substitutes" section, followed by §7 Conclusion (~8 lines), then §8 Limitations (~20 lines) before References begin.

So page 9 contains roughly 1.5 columns of Discussion and early conclusion material before Limitations takes over around the 40% mark on the right column. To get this down to 8 pages, I need to cut approximately 1.5 columns of text—that's substantial compression needed. Let me think through where I can trim without losing the core narrative.

The Discussion section is the biggest target. Those new paragraphs I added about KV cache drift, PCGrad magnitude, and the GAR comparison are running about 30 lines and could be condensed significantly. I should also scan for repeated numbers throughout the paper that could be deduplicated, and the contribution list could drop from 5 items to 4 if needed. Beyond that, I'll look for any verbose passages that can be tightened up.

Let me start by reviewing all the main text sections to spot where the compression opportunities actually are. I need to be precise about this—cutting roughly 60 lines total across the entire paper without gutting the arguments.

The Discussion is definitely where I can make the biggest impact. The opening paragraph runs about 8 lines, the "Why NTP self-recovers but GAR does not" section is around 10 lines and could fold into the opening, the "Alternative explanations" part is about 12 lines but could compress to 5 or 6, and the other subsections are already fairly tight. That's probably 15-20 lines of savings right there.

The Analysis section has multiple places to trim. The gate conditioning subsection has detailed gate values I could pare down, PCGrad has verbose gradient statistics, the distribution shift discussion could be leaner, and that long Llama steering paragraph in the cross-model section is a candidate for compression. I'm looking at maybe 10-15 lines of potential savings there.

The Experiments section has the steering equation plus text and a very long steering paragraph that could both be condensed—probably 5-10 lines. The Method section's gate network description and loss design evolution paragraph are verbose enough to save another 5-10 lines. The Introduction could lose a few lines from the contributions statement, and I should scan all sections for repeated numbers or statistics that could be deduplicated.

I'm targeting around 60 lines total, and this breakdown suggests I can hit that by starting with Discussion, then moving through Analysis, Experiments, Method, and Introduction in order of impact.
