3	Multi-turn dialogue is central to how large language models are deployed, yet a persistent failure accompanies even the most fluent generation: as conversations grow longer, models pay progressively less attention to the system-prompt goals that define their task. \citet{dongre2026attention} measured 27--48\% Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) decline across 12 models over 50 turns, even as residual stream probes confirm that goal representations persist (AUC\,=\,0.98). This is not just a measurement artifact---exploratory evaluation suggests it can degrade instruction compliance by up to 24 percentage points on content-constrained tasks (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
4	
5	Existing remedies address this decay at inference time. Split-softmax \citep{li2024measuring} separates instruction from conversation tokens; StreamingLLM \citep{xiao2024streaming} retains anchor tokens; Gated Attention \citep{qiu2025gated} applies per-head sigmoid gates within a single forward pass. None of these intervenes during training. A natural alternative is to optimize goal attention directly through auxiliary attention losses trained alongside next-token prediction (NTP). Yet this strategy rests on an untested assumption: that attention patterns learned under teacher forcing transfer to autoregressive generation. Classical exposure bias research \citep{bengio2015scheduled,lamb2016professor} has shown that teacher forcing creates a training--inference mismatch for RNN sequence models, but whether this mismatch extends to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers remains unknown.
6	
7	We test this assumption. We introduce ThreadGate, per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned gates on the residual stream ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l)$), designed not as a solution but as a controlled testbed for isolating failure mechanisms. A five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) forms a causal exclusion chain (\S\ref{sec:comparison-design}): each adjacent pair rules out one alternative hypothesis---turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict---tracing the failure to its root cause.
8	
9	\begin{figure*}[t]
10	  \centering
11	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_headline.pdf}
12	  \caption{Exposure bias destroys auxiliary attention losses. \textbf{(a)}~Goal attention decays 85--90\% across turns under AR generation on both Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B. \textbf{(b)}~Under TF evaluation all variants reach GAR$\approx$0.50, but AR generation collapses GAR by 8--17$\times$ (log scale). \textbf{(c)}~Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 7.8$\times$ to 1.3$\times$ (Qwen) and 12.0$\times$ to 2.9$\times$ (Llama).}
13	  \label{fig:headline}
14	\end{figure*}
15	
16	The result is clear: training succeeds but inference fails. All variants converge to TF GAR\,=\,0.50, yet under autoregressive generation GAR collapses to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\times$ gap on Qwen-2.5-7B and 27$\times$ on Llama-3.1-8B (Figure~\ref{fig:headline}). The causal exclusion chain identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor consistent with all evidence: PCGrad removes gradient conflict yet \emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\,=\,0.002), ruling it out by \emph{modus tollens}; gating provides no benefit over LoRA-only. Crucially, inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming that the collapse reflects attention misallocation rather than representation degradation. Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ on Qwen and ${\sim}$2.9$\times$ on Llama with no perplexity cost---the first application of scheduled sampling to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers.
17	
18	Our contributions: (1)~the first empirical characterization of exposure bias on auxiliary attention losses (8--27$\times$ TF--AR gap across two architectures); (2)~a five-way diagnostic testbed informed by seven loss design iterations, enabling causal exclusion of gradient conflict via PCGrad ($p$\,=\,0.002, effect reverses); (3)~inference-time $\beta$-steering recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause; (4)~cross-architecture validation on Llama-3.1-8B and cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B; (5)~scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 8--27$\times$ to 1.3--2.9$\times$ ($0.401\pm0.047$, three seeds on Qwen), the first transfer to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers. Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/turn-residual-gating}.
19	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\looseness=-1 The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP self-recovers because the task (predict one token from a prefix) is identical at training and inference \citep{he2019exposure}; GAR optimizes a \emph{global property of the prefix}---attention allocation to goal tokens---that has no autoregressive counterpart. Per-head analysis confirms this selectivity: heads with higher TF GAR shift most under AR generation ($r$\,=\,0.743; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-kl}). Any auxiliary attention objective paired with teacher forcing should face the same barrier when targeting distributional properties rather than the prediction itself.
4	
5	\paragraph{Why NTP self-recovers but GAR does not.} NTP self-recovers because prediction is the same task at both regimes; exposure bias shifts the \emph{input distribution} but not the \emph{loss function}. GAR optimizes a global property of attention flow through layers \citep{abnar2020quantifying} over model-generated tokens that has no training-time analogue, explaining why the TF--AR gap is order-of-magnitude for GAR-ours but negligible for NTP. Crucially, the collapse is selective: GAR-ours (last-token attention) collapses 8--27$\times$ while GAR-Dongre (all-token attention) shows negligible shift (${\leq}$1.3$\times$). This asymmetry implies that the training-time GAR signal concentrates attention at a specific position (last token of each turn), a pattern that is brittle under distribution shift; the diffuse attention measured by GAR-Dongre is robust precisely because it was never specifically optimized.
6	
7	\paragraph{Alternative explanations.} Our causal exclusion identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor consistent with all evidence, but we cannot rule out all alternatives. KV cache drift---the progressive corruption of cached key-value representations as model-generated tokens replace ground-truth tokens---could compound exposure bias or operate independently. Disentangling the two would require interventions that selectively reset KV cache states at turn boundaries, which we leave to future work. The PCGrad exclusion rules out gradient \emph{direction} conflict but not magnitude imbalance: NTP magnitude dominance grows from 7.5$\times$ to 14.7$\times$ across epochs (25.7$\times$ on Llama), meaning the GAR gradient is effectively suppressed even when not conflicting. Whether magnitude rebalancing (e.g., GradNorm; \citealp{chen2018gradnorm}) would improve AR transfer remains untested.
8	
9	\paragraph{Downstream effects.} Instruction retention ($\beta$\,=\,3) shows late-turn compliance rising from 75.9\% to 87.0\% (+11.1\,pp, $p$\,=\,0.077; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ir}). Content constraints benefit most (+23.8\,pp), consistent with GAR targeting cross-turn semantic coherence rather than locally-verifiable procedural rules. However, the overall correlation between GAR-ours and instruction retention is weak ($r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007; $r^2$\,=\,0.04), reinforcing the interpretation of GAR as a diagnostic probe rather than a direct optimization target: high TF GAR is a necessary signal that the loss is working but not a sufficient predictor of downstream compliance.
10	
11	\paragraph{SS and steering as substitutes.} \looseness=-1 SS provides training-time mitigation with no inference cost; steering provides inference-time recovery with PPL cost. At $\beta$\,=\,5 the two converge (SS GAR\,=\,0.717 vs.\ TG\,=\,0.727). SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.
12	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	\subsection{Preliminaries}\label{sec:background}
4	
5	\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}
6	Standard metrics such as perplexity and attention entropy capture generation quality but not \emph{what} the model attends to. In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \emph{goal tokens} $\mathcal{G}$. In the standard transformer attention mechanism \citep{vaswani2017attention}, \citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\tau$ as
7	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar}
8	  \text{GAR}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H \cdot |\mathcal{R}_\tau|} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{R}_\tau} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)},
9	\end{equation}
10	where $\mathcal{R}_\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\ell$, head $h$.
11	
12	\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}
13	\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. However, averaging dilutes the signal with intermediate positions that may legitimately attend to local context rather than the system prompt. The last token of each assistant turn is where the model aggregates information for the next continuation---attention at this position most directly reflects the model's awareness of task specifications. \textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement accordingly:
14	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar-ours}
15	  \text{GAR\text{-}ours}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{t_\tau,\, j}^{(\ell,h)},
16	\end{equation}
17	where $t_\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ (\S\ref{sec:loss}). Throughout this work, GAR serves primarily as a \emph{diagnostic probe}: it measures whether auxiliary attention training transfers to autoregressive generation, rather than being an end-to-end optimization objective whose increase directly improves downstream task performance (the correlation with instruction retention is weak; $r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
18	
19	\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}
20	
21	\begin{figure}[t]
22	  \centering
23	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}
24	  \caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\,=\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\% decay; $\Delta$\,=\,3.3\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect.}
25	  \label{fig:attention-heatmap}
26	\end{figure}
27	
28	Different transformer layers serve different roles: early layers capture local syntax while later layers integrate long-range context \citep{gromov2024unreasonable}. A global intervention would uniformly scale all layers, but goal attention decay is layer-dependent: some layers contribute more to cross-turn information routing than others. ThreadGate therefore adds a \emph{per-layer} gating mechanism to the residual stream, allowing the model to learn which layers to modulate. At each layer $l$, the residual update is scaled by a scalar gate:
29	\begin{equation}
30	  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l),
31	\end{equation}
32	where $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or feed-forward network, FFN). From the perspective that residual networks function as ensembles of shallow paths \citep{veit2016residual}, per-layer gating selectively modulates individual paths. This resembles ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training. ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \emph{content-conditioned}, a function of the hidden state rather than a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \emph{turn-conditioned}, incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\sigma(1.5) \approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.
33	
34	\paragraph{Gate network.} A fixed scalar per layer (as in ReZero) cannot adapt to varying inputs; the gate must be \emph{content-conditioned} so that different conversations or turns can receive different modulation. Adding \emph{turn conditioning} allows the gate to distinguish early turns (where GAR is naturally high) from late turns (where intervention is most needed). Each gate $g_l$ is thus produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:
35	\begin{align}\label{eq:gate}
36	  g_l = \sigma\bigl(W_2\,\text{ReLU}(&W_1\,[\text{proj}(x_l);\;\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \notag\\
37	  &+ b_1) + b_2\bigr),
38	\end{align}
39	where $\text{proj}(\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\!=\!28$), this adds ${\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\% of 7B).
40	
41	\paragraph{Turn embedding.} The embedding $\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ encodes turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables, allowing gates to distinguish conversation positions. The \textbf{Uniform Gate} ablation replaces $\text{emb}(\cdot)$ with a learnable constant, removing turn awareness while retaining a learnable content-conditioned MLP gate.
42	
43	\paragraph{Initialization.} Gate biases are set to 1.5 (${\sim}$82\% pass-through), contrasting ReZero's zero initialization that would suppress pretrained capabilities. Gate parameters receive a $10\times$ learning rate multiplier.
44	
45	\subsection{Training Objective}\label{sec:loss}
46	
47	Next-token prediction alone provides no gradient signal for attention allocation; a model can achieve low perplexity while distributing attention arbitrarily. The auxiliary GAR loss provides explicit pressure to maintain goal-token attention, while a diversity regularizer prevents gate collapse. The training objective combines three terms:
48	\begin{equation}
49	  \mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \lambda_\text{GAR}\,\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} + \lambda_\text{div}\,\mathcal{L}_\text{div}.
50	\end{equation}
51	$\mathcal{L}_\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. We use mean squared error (MSE) rather than cross-entropy for $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ because GAR is a continuous ratio, not a probability, and MSE provides \emph{bidirectional} pressure, penalizing both under-attention (GAR\,$<$\,$\tau$) and over-attention (GAR\,$>$\,$\tau$). This symmetry is critical: a unidirectional maximize objective drives gates toward identity (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-loss}). The GAR loss is:
52	\begin{equation}
53	  \mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} = \text{MSE}(\text{GAR\text{-}ours}, \tau),
54	\end{equation}
55	where $\tau = 0.5$ targets allocating half of the last token's attention capacity to goal tokens. GAR is measured at the last token of each assistant turn (GAR-ours, \S\ref{sec:background}). Without explicit diversity pressure, all gates converge to the same value (typically near 1.0), eliminating the layer-specific modulation that is the design intent. The gate diversity loss prevents this collapse:
56	\begin{equation}
57	  \mathcal{L}_\text{div} = \text{ReLU}(\sigma_\text{min} - \text{std}(\mathbf{g})),
58	\end{equation}
59	where $\mathbf{g} = [g_1, \ldots, g_L]$ and $\sigma_\text{min} = 0.05$ is a minimum standard deviation threshold. Both $\lambda_\text{GAR} = \lambda_\text{div} = 1.0$.
60	
61	\paragraph{Loss design evolution.} The MSE formulation was selected from seven iterations spanning four loss families (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-loss}): cumulative (v1--v2) and maximize (v3--v6) objectives either starve gate gradients at equilibrium or drive gates toward identity. Only MSE provides bidirectional pressure, penalizing both over- and under-attention, preventing identity collapse.
62	
63	\subsection{Controlled Comparison Design}\label{sec:comparison-design}
64	
65	We design a five-way comparison where each adjacent pair isolates one factor: \textbf{ThreadGate (TG) vs.\ Uniform Gate (UG)} (turn conditioning), \textbf{TG vs.\ LoRA-only} (gating mechanism), \textbf{ThreadGate vs.\ +PCGrad} (gradient conflict via gradient surgery \citep{yu2020pcgrad}), and \textbf{Fine-tuned vs.\ Base} (LoRA adaptation). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}).
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	  \centering
69	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}
70	  \caption{Inference-time $\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause (9\% PPL cost at $\beta$\,=\,3; phase transition at $\beta \in [2, 3]$).}
71	  \label{fig:steering-recovery}
72	\end{figure}
73

In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \\emph{goal tokens} $\\mathcal{G}$. In the standard transformer attention mechanism \\citep{vaswani2017attention}, \\citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\\tau$ as\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar}\n  \\text{GAR}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H \\cdot |\\mathcal{R}_\\tau|} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{i \\in \\mathcal{R}_\\tau} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathcal{R}_\\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\\ell$, head $h$.\n\n\\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}\n\\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. ```text
1	\section{Analysis}\label{sec:analysis}
2	
3	The five-way comparison (\S\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain; each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \S\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \S\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \S\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.
4	
5	\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}
6	
7	We compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$); e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, a convergence pattern reminiscent of attention collapse in deep transformers \citep{dong2021attention}, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\% attenuation; Table~\ref{tab:four-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\,=\,0.777, L27\,=\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?
8	
9	\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\label{subsec:pcgrad}
10	
11	NTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\times$ to 14.7$\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane, should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.
12	
13	PCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \emph{worsens}: 0.064$\to$0.030 ($p$\,=\,0.002; Figure~\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). By \emph{modus tollens}: removing gradient conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\times$ magnitude dominance renders gradient conflict inconsequential. Cross-model analysis confirms this pattern: Llama-3.1-8B gradients are near-orthogonal (cos\,=\,+0.005) with 25.7$\times$ NTP dominance (Table~\ref{tab:gradient-crossmodel}).
14	
15	\begin{figure}[t]
16	  \centering
17	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_ss_multiseed.pdf}
18	  \caption{Multi-seed AR GAR under scheduled sampling ($\beta$\,=\,0 and $\beta$\,=\,5, three seeds). Steering nearly eliminates seed variance (std: 0.047$\to$0.001).}
19	  \label{fig:ss-multiseed}
20	\end{figure}
21	
22	\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\label{subsec:dist-shift}
23	
24	The TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Unlike token-level predictions, attention flow through layers \citep{abnar2020quantifying} over model-generated tokens has no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\tau$\,=\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\% per-turn decay; Figure~\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}; per-turn dynamics in Appendices~\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics} and~\ref{sec:appendix-perturn-detail}).
25	
26	\begin{figure}[t]
27	  \centering
28	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}
29	  \caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\,=\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants. Pilot gaps (20--34$\times$) exceed final $N$\,=\,50 estimates (8--17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:four-way}) due to smaller sample size.}
30	  \label{fig:gate-shift}
31	\end{figure}
32	
33	\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}
34	
35	We test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\times$) vs.\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.
36	
37	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\label{subsec:cross-model}
38	
39	We replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\,=\,20, 4 turns). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) / 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (coefficient of variation, CoV\,$>$\,100\%); we report medians: 0.018 (TG and LoRA-only), 0.009 (Base), yielding gaps of 27$\times$ and 26$\times$ (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}a in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-llama}). Gate profiles differ qualitatively (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\,=\,0.793) while Llama dips at late layers (L25\,=\,0.719), an architecture-dependent pattern, but convergence toward identity is universal (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\times$ on Qwen, 27$\times$ on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as architecture-independent.
40	
41	\enlargethispage{2\baselineskip}
42	Scheduled sampling confirms cross-model generalization: Llama SS compresses the gap from ${\sim}$12$\times$ to 2.9$\times$ (TF GAR 0.477, AR GAR 0.166), weaker than Qwen's ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\ 3). The phase transition is architecture-dependent: $\beta$\,=\,3 triggers recovery on Qwen (0.556) but not Llama (0.173); Llama requires $\beta$\,=\,5 (GAR 0.791, exceeding Qwen SS 0.717), suggesting deeper architectures need higher steering thresholds but ultimately provide more surface for attention reweighting (full results in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-llama}). Cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B confirms the same pattern: 79.8\% GAR decay and $\beta$-steering recovery to 0.715 (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-1.5b}).
```

This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain; each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$); e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, a convergence pattern reminiscent of attention collapse in deep transformers \\citep{dong2021attention}, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane, should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing gradient conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ magnitude dominance renders gradient conflict inconsequential. Cross-model analysis confirms this pattern: Llama-3.1-8B gradients are near-orthogonal (cos\\,=\\,+0.005) with 25.7$\\times$ NTP dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:gradient-crossmodel}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_multiseed.pdf}\n  \\caption{Multi-seed AR GAR under scheduled sampling ($\\beta$\\,=\\,0 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, three seeds). Steering nearly eliminates seed variance (std: 0.047$\\to$0.001).}\n  \\label{fig:ss-multiseed}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Unlike token-level predictions, attention flow through layers \\citep{abnar2020quantifying} over model-generated tokens has no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}; per-turn dynamics in Appendices~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics} and~\\ref{sec:appendix-perturn-detail}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants. Pilot gaps (20--34$\\times$) exceed final $N$\\,=\\,50 estimates (8--17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}) due to smaller sample size.}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20, 4 turns). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) / 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (coefficient of variation, CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%); we report medians: 0.018 (TG and LoRA-only), 0.009 (Base), yielding gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}). Gate profiles differ qualitatively (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793) while Llama dips at late layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), an architecture-dependent pattern, but convergence toward identity is universal (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\\times$ on Qwen, 27$\\times$ on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as architecture-independent.\n\n\\enlargethispage{2\\baselineskip}\nScheduled sampling confirms cross-model generalization: Llama SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$12$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (TF GAR 0.477, AR GAR 0.166), weaker than Qwen's ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3). The phase transition is architecture-dependent: $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 triggers recovery on Qwen (0.556) but not Llama (0.173); Llama requires $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 (GAR 0.791, exceeding Qwen SS 0.717), suggesting deeper architectures need higher steering thresholds but ultimately provide more surface for attention reweighting (full results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}). Cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B confirms the same pattern: 79.8\\% GAR decay and $\\beta$-steering recovery to 0.715 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-1.5b}).",

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	
6	We fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct with LoRA \citep{hu2022lora,dettmers2023qlora} on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat \citep{ding2023ultrachat} training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; dataset statistics in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-data}; the controlled comparison design is described in \S\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention}, and perplexity (PPL).
7	
8	\subsection{Main Results}
9	
10	Table~\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\leq$2\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. IID and held-out protocols produce identical rankings (held-out GAR-ours differs by $\leq$1\,pp), ruling out data overlap as a confound. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02) with no TF-AR gap, confirming that only concentrated last-token attention is vulnerable to distribution shift; scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\S\ref{subsec:ss}).
11	
12	Under AR generation ($N$\,=\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\times$ gap (Figure~\ref{fig:headline}). The ordering (ThreadGate 0.064 $>$ LoRA-only 0.037 $>$ Uniform Gate 0.033 $>$ PCGrad 0.030 $>$ Base 0.019) shows ThreadGate vs.\ LoRA-only is significant ($p$\,=\,0.004; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-stats}), yet the gap remains 7.8$\times$. A second seed confirms TF stability ($0.504\pm0.001$). MMLU 5-shot accuracy remains within 0.8\,pp across all variants (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-mmlu}). We analyze this distribution shift in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
13	
14	\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\label{subsec:steering}
15	
16	\begin{figure}[t]
17	  \centering
18	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_ss_gap_compression.pdf}
19	  \caption{Scheduled sampling compresses the TF-AR gap from 7.8$\times$ to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$, the only variant approaching parity across five training conditions.}
20	  \label{fig:ss-gap}
21	\end{figure}
22	
23	All training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:
24	\begin{equation}
25	  \tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)} + \beta \cdot \mathbf{1}[j \in \mathcal{G}],
26	\end{equation}
27	where $\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero. The response is sharply nonlinear: $\beta \in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\,=\,0.556 (9\% PPL cost). At $\beta$\,=\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\beta \in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Figure~\ref{fig:steering-recovery}; full results in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-steering}; per-$\beta$ turn-level profiles in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-steering-viz}).
28	
29	This closes the causal loop: if the collapse were due to representation drift in hidden states, no amount of attention reweighting could recover GAR. The recovery confirms attention misallocation, not representation degradation, as the proximal cause (qualitative examples in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-examples}).
30	
31	\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\label{subsec:ss}
32	
33	Scheduled sampling (SS; \citealp{bengio2015scheduled}), which mixes model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability (implementation details in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ss}), directly targets the training-inference mismatch identified in \S\ref{subsec:dist-shift}. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises from 0.064 to $0.401\pm0.047$ (seeds: 0.347 / 0.423 / 0.433; per-seed details in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-perseed}), compressing the gap from 7.8$\times$ to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ (per-seed gaps: 1.45$\times$, 1.17$\times$, 1.15$\times$; TF GAR $0.498\pm0.004$) with no PPL cost ($1.441\pm0.001$; Figure~\ref{fig:ss-gap}). Steering on SS models is remarkably stable ($\beta$\,=\,5: $0.717\pm0.001$): the training-time seed variance (std\,=\,0.047 at $\beta$\,=\,0) is nearly eliminated (Figure~\ref{fig:ss-multiseed}), suggesting $\beta$-steering converges to a seed-independent attention equilibrium. The improvement generalizes across GAR definitions: SS raises mid-turn GAR above 0.36 under all four alternative token-position variants (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gar-robust}), while ThreadGate only improves last-token GAR (0.325 vs.\ all-token 0.025): SS builds a broad goal-attention pattern, not position-specific overfitting. GAR-Dongre also rises substantially under SS ($N$\,=\,5 pilot; see Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), the only intervention that moves both metrics. The benefit generalizes across architectures: on Llama-3.1-8B, SS compresses the gap from ${\sim}$12$\times$ to 2.9$\times$ (AR GAR 0.166, TF GAR 0.477), weaker than Qwen's ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\ 3; \S\ref{subsec:cross-model}). The benefit diminishes in longer dialogues (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), consistent with exposure bias compounding over turns.
34	
35	
36	
```

- Condense the ReZero comparison from 4 lines to 1-2
- Remove repeated motivation from gate network paragraph
- Move loss design evolution to a reference

**7. Deduplication — Compress ~5 lines**
- Eliminate redundant numbers across sections

Now I'm executing these compressions, starting with the biggest savings opportunities.

For the Discussion section, I'm merging the opening paragraph with the self-recovery explanation into one tight paragraph that explains the core incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR losses, then shows how the collapse is selective based on attention patterns—keeping the key empirical details about the 8–27× shift versus negligible changes, and the per-head analysis that reveals which heads are most brittle.

Now I need to compress the alternative explanations paragraph, which acknowledges that KV cache drift could be a confounding factor but remains untested, while also noting that PCGrad rules out gradient direction conflicts but not magnitude imbalance given NTP's dominance.

Then I'm condensing the downstream effects section, which shows how steering with β=3 improves late-turn compliance. ); (3) inference-time β-steering pinpoints attention misallocation as the root cause; (4) scheduled sampling narrows the gap to 1.3–2.9×, marking the first successful transfer of this technique to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers.

This consolidates the contributions by folding architectural breadth into the first point and streamlining each item. The summary could tighten further by eliminating redundant metrics and using cross-references instead.

For the distribution shift section, I can remove the explanation of why NTP self-recovers—that's already covered in the discussion—and just focus on the key finding that GAR optimizes a distributional property without an autoregressive equivalent, which is what drives the divergence.

In the experiments section, I'll strip out the per-seed variance numbers and move those details to the appendix, keeping only the reference to where readers can find them. Steering on SS models nearly eliminates seed variance across different GAR definitions, with mid-turn performance staying above 0.36 across all token-position variants, whereas ThreadGate only improves last-token GAR—suggesting SS builds a broad goal-attention pattern rather than position-specific overfitting. The GAR-Dongre metric also rises substantially under SS, making it the only intervention that improves both metrics.

For the method section, I'm trimming the gate network motivation down to just the core definition, removing the redundant symmetry statement since the loss design already covers that, and consolidating the steering paragraphs by merging the causal loop explanation into a single sentence that ties attention misallocation directly to the PPL results.
```

  "content": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\looseness=-1 The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP self-recovers because prediction is the same task at both regimes \\citep{he2019exposure}; GAR optimizes a \\emph{global property}---attention allocation to goal tokens---that has no autoregressive counterpart. The collapse is selective: GAR-ours (last-token) collapses 8--27$\\times$ while GAR-Dongre (all-token) shows negligible shift (${\\leq}$1.3$\\times$), implying the concentrated attention pattern is brittle under distribution shift. Per-head analysis confirms: heads with higher TF GAR shift most under AR generation ($r$\\,=\\,0.743; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-kl}). Any auxiliary attention objective paired with teacher forcing should face the same barrier when targeting distributional properties rather than the prediction itself.\n\n\\paragraph{Alternative explanations.} Our exclusion identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor, but KV cache drift---progressive corruption of cached representations as model-generated tokens replace ground-truth---could compound or operate independently and remains untested. The PCGrad exclusion rules out gradient \\emph{direction} conflict but not magnitude imbalance (NTP dominance 7.5--14.7$\\times$, 25.7$\\times$ on Llama; \\citealp{chen2018gradnorm}).\n\n\\paragraph{Downstream effects.} Steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3) raises late-turn compliance by +11.1\\,pp ($p$\\,=\\,0.077), with content constraints benefiting most (+23.8\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}). The overall GAR--compliance correlation is weak ($r$\\,=\\,0.200, $r^2$\\,=\\,0.04), reinforcing GAR's role as a diagnostic probe rather than a direct optimization target.\n\n\\paragraph{SS and steering as substitutes.} \\looseness=-1 SS provides training-time mitigation with no inference cost; steering provides inference-time recovery with PPL cost. At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 the two converge (SS GAR\\,=\\,0.717 vs.\\ TG\\,=\\,0.727). SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.\n"
}

The causal exclusion identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor: PCGrad removes gradient conflict yet \\emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\\,=\\,0.002); gating provides no benefit over LoRA-only. Steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation; scheduled sampling compresses the gap to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ (Qwen) and ${\\sim}$2.9$\\times$ (Llama) with no perplexity cost.\n\nOur contributions: (1)~the first empirical characterization of exposure bias on auxiliary attention losses (8--27$\\times$ gap across two architectures and scales); (2)~a five-way diagnostic testbed informed by seven loss iterations, enabling causal exclusion of gradient conflict via PCGrad ($p$\\,=\\,0.002, effect reverses); (3)~$\\beta$-steering confirms attention misallocation as the proximal cause; (4)~scheduled sampling compresses the gap to 1.3--2.9$\\times$, the first transfer to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers ($0.401\\pm0.047$, three seeds). Code and data: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/turn-residual-gating}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is central to how large language models are deployed, yet a persistent failure accompanies even the most fluent generation: as conversations grow longer, models pay progressively less attention to the system-prompt goals that define their task. \\citet{dongre2026attention} measured 27--48\\% Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) decline across 12 models over 50 turns, even as residual stream probes confirm that goal representations persist (AUC\\,=\\,0.98). This is not just a measurement artifact---exploratory evaluation suggests it can degrade instruction compliance by up to 24 percentage points on content-constrained tasks (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nExisting remedies address this decay at inference time. Split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring} separates instruction from conversation tokens; StreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} retains anchor tokens; Gated Attention \\citep{qiu2025gated} applies per-head sigmoid gates within a single forward pass. None of these intervenes during training. A natural alternative is to optimize goal attention directly through auxiliary attention losses trained alongside next-token prediction (NTP). Yet this strategy rests on an untested assumption: that attention patterns learned under teacher forcing transfer to autoregressive generation. Classical exposure bias research \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,lamb2016professor} has shown that teacher forcing creates a training--inference mismatch for RNN sequence models, but whether this mismatch extends to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers remains unknown.\n\nWe test this assumption. We introduce ThreadGate, per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned gates on the residual stream ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$), designed not as a solution but as a controlled testbed for isolating failure mechanisms. A five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) forms a causal exclusion chain (\\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}): each adjacent pair rules out one alternative hypothesis---turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict---tracing the failure to its root cause.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_headline.pdf}\n  \\caption{Exposure bias destroys auxiliary attention losses. \\textbf{(a)}~Goal attention decays 85--90\\% across turns under AR generation on both Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B. \\textbf{(b)}~Under TF evaluation all variants reach GAR$\\approx$0.50, but AR generation collapses GAR by 8--17$\\times$ (log scale). \\textbf{(c)}~Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to 1.3$\\times$ (Qwen) and 12.0$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (Llama).}\n  \\label{fig:headline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThe result is clear: training succeeds but inference fails. All variants converge to TF GAR\\,=\\,0.50, yet under autoregressive generation GAR collapses to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap on Qwen-2.5-7B and 27$\\times$ on Llama-3.1-8B (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}). The causal exclusion chain identifies exposure bias as the primary contributing factor consistent with all evidence: PCGrad removes gradient conflict yet \\emph{worsens} AR GAR ($p$\\,=\\,0.002), ruling it out by \\emph{modus tollens}; gating provides no benefit over LoRA-only. Crucially, inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming that the collapse reflects attention misallocation rather than representation degradation. Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ on Qwen and ${\\sim}$2.9$\\times$ on Llama with no perplexity cost---the first application of scheduled sampling to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers.\n\nOur contributions: (1)~the first empirical characterization of exposure bias on auxiliary attention losses (8--27$\\times$ TF--AR gap across two architectures); (2)~a five-way diagnostic testbed informed by seven loss design iterations, enabling causal exclusion of gradient conflict via PCGrad ($p$\\,=\\,0.002, effect reverses); (3)~inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause; (4)~cross-architecture validation on Llama-3.1-8B and cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B; (5)~scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 8--27$\\times$ to 1.3--2.9$\\times$ ($0.401\\pm0.047$, three seeds on Qwen), the first transfer to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers. Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/turn-residual-gating}.\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "The TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Unlike token-level predictions, attention flow through layers \\citep{abnar2020quantifying} over model-generated tokens has no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}; per-turn dynamics in Appendices~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics} and~\\ref{sec:appendix-perturn-detail}).",
  "new_string": "The TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, confirming that the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling})."

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain; each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$); e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane, should prevent the collapse. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Unlike token-level predictions, attention flow through layers \\citep{abnar2020quantifying} over model-generated tokens has no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}; per-turn dynamics in Appendices~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics} and~\\ref{sec:appendix-perturn-detail}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21.     "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct with LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora,dettmers2023qlora} on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat \\citep{ding2023ultrachat} training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; dataset statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-data}; the controlled comparison design is described in \\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and perplexity (PPL).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\\leq$2\\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. IID and held-out protocols produce identical rankings (held-out GAR-ours differs by $\\leq$1\\,pp), ruling out data overlap as a confound. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02) with no TF-AR gap, confirming that only concentrated last-token attention is vulnerable to distribution shift; scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{subsec:ss}).\n\nUnder AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}). The ordering (ThreadGate 0.064 $>$ LoRA-only 0.037 $>$ Uniform Gate 0.033 $>$ PCGrad 0.030 $>$ Base 0.019) shows ThreadGate vs.\\ LoRA-only is significant ($p$\\,=\\,0.004; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-stats}), yet the gap remains 7.8$\\times$. A second seed confirms TF stability ($0.504\\pm0.001$). MMLU 5-shot accuracy remains within 0.8\\,pp across all variants (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-mmlu}). We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_gap_compression.pdf}\n  \\caption{Scheduled sampling compresses the TF-AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$, the only variant approaching parity across five training conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:ss-gap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAll training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} + \\beta \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[j \\in \\mathcal{G}],\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero. The response is sharply nonlinear: $\\beta \\in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\\,=\\,0.556 (9\\% PPL cost). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\\beta \\in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering-recovery}; full results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering}; per-$\\beta$ turn-level profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering-viz}).\n\nThis closes the causal loop: if the collapse were due to representation drift in hidden states, no amount of attention reweighting could recover GAR. The recovery confirms attention misallocation, not representation degradation, as the proximal cause (qualitative examples in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-examples}).\n\n\\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\\label{subsec:ss}\n\nScheduled sampling (SS; \\citealp{bengio2015scheduled}), which mixes model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability (implementation details in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ss}), directly targets the training-inference mismatch identified in \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift}. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises from 0.064 to $0.401\\pm0.047$ (seeds: 0.347 / 0.423 / 0.433; per-seed details in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-perseed}), compressing the gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ (per-seed gaps: 1.45$\\times$, 1.17$\\times$, 1.15$\\times$; TF GAR $0.498\\pm0.004$) with no PPL cost ($1.441\\pm0.001$; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-gap}). Steering on SS models is remarkably stable ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: $0.717\\pm0.001$): the training-time seed variance (std\\,=\\,0.047 at $\\beta$\\,=\\,0) is nearly eliminated (Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-multiseed}), suggesting $\\beta$-steering converges to a seed-independent attention equilibrium. The improvement generalizes across GAR definitions: SS raises mid-turn GAR above 0.36 under all four alternative token-position variants (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-robust}), while ThreadGate only improves last-token GAR (0.325 vs.\\ all-token 0.025): SS builds a broad goal-attention pattern, not position-specific overfitting.     "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct with LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora,dettmers2023qlora} on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat \\citep{ding2023ultrachat} training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; dataset statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-data}; the controlled comparison design is described in \\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and perplexity (PPL).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\\leq$2\\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. IID and held-out protocols produce identical rankings (held-out GAR-ours differs by $\\leq$1\\,pp), ruling out data overlap as a confound. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02) with no TF-AR gap, confirming that only concentrated last-token attention is vulnerable to distribution shift; scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{subsec:ss}).\n\nUnder AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}). The ordering (ThreadGate 0.064 $>$ LoRA-only 0.037 $>$ Uniform Gate 0.033 $>$ PCGrad 0.030 $>$ Base 0.019) shows ThreadGate vs.\\ LoRA-only is significant ($p$\\,=\\,0.004; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-stats}), yet the gap remains 7.8$\\times$. A second seed confirms TF stability ($0.504\\pm0.001$). MMLU 5-shot accuracy remains within 0.8\\,pp across all variants (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-mmlu}). We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_gap_compression.pdf}\n  \\caption{Scheduled sampling compresses the TF-AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$, the only variant approaching parity across five training conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:ss-gap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAll training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} + \\beta \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[j \\in \\mathcal{G}],\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero. The response is sharply nonlinear: $\\beta \\in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\\,=\\,0.556 (9\\% PPL cost). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\\beta \\in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering-recovery}; full results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering}). The recovery confirms attention misallocation, not representation degradation, as the proximal cause: if the collapse were due to hidden-state drift, attention reweighting could not recover GAR (qualitative examples in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-examples}).\n\n\\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\\label{subsec:ss}\n\nScheduled sampling (SS; \\citealp{bengio2015scheduled}), which mixes model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability (implementation details in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ss}), directly targets the training-inference mismatch identified in \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift}. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises from 0.064 to $0.401\\pm0.047$ (seeds: 0.347 / 0.423 / 0.433; per-seed details in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-perseed}), compressing the gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ (per-seed gaps: 1.45$\\times$, 1.17$\\times$, 1.15$\\times$; TF GAR $0.498\\pm0.004$) with no PPL cost ($1.441\\pm0.001$; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-gap}). Steering on SS models is remarkably stable ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: $0.717\\pm0.001$): the training-time seed variance (std\\,=\\,0.047 at $\\beta$\\,=\\,0) is nearly eliminated (Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-multiseed}), suggesting $\\beta$-steering converges to a seed-independent attention equilibrium. The improvement generalizes across GAR definitions: SS raises mid-turn GAR above 0.36 under all four alternative token-position variants (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-robust}), while ThreadGate only improves last-token GAR (0.325 vs.\\ all-token 0.025): SS builds a broad goal-attention pattern, not position-specific overfitting. GAR-Dongre also rises substantially under SS ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot; see Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), the only intervention that moves both metrics. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises to $0.401\\pm0.047$, compressing the gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ (TF GAR $0.498\\pm0.004$) with no PPL cost ($1.441\\pm0.001$; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-gap}; per-seed details in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-perseed}). Steering on SS models nearly eliminates seed variance ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: $0.717\\pm0.001$, std 0.047$\\to$0.001; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-multiseed}). SS generalizes across GAR definitions (mid-turn GAR above 0.36 for all token-position variants; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-robust}) and across architectures: on Llama-3.1-8B, SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$12$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$, weaker than Qwen likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3; \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model}). The benefit diminishes in longer dialogues (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), consistent with exposure bias compounding over turns.",

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Gate network.} A fixed scalar per layer (as in ReZero) cannot adapt to varying inputs; the gate must be \\emph{content-conditioned} so that different conversations or turns can receive different modulation. Adding \\emph{turn conditioning} allows the gate to distinguish early turns (where GAR is naturally high) from late turns (where intervention is most needed). Each gate $g_l$ is thus produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Gate network.} Each gate $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Preliminaries}\\label{sec:background}\n\n\\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}\nStandard metrics such as perplexity and attention entropy capture generation quality but not \\emph{what} the model attends to. In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \\emph{goal tokens} $\\mathcal{G}$. In the standard transformer attention mechanism \\citep{vaswani2017attention}, \\citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\\tau$ as\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar}\n  \\text{GAR}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H \\cdot |\\mathcal{R}_\\tau|} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{i \\in \\mathcal{R}_\\tau} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathcal{R}_\\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\\ell$, head $h$.\n\n\\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}\n\\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. However, averaging dilutes the signal with intermediate positions that may legitimately attend to local context rather than the system prompt. The last token of each assistant turn is where the model aggregates information for the next continuation---attention at this position most directly reflects the model's awareness of task specifications. \\textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement accordingly:\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar-ours}\n  \\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{t_\\tau,\\, j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $t_\\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). Throughout this work, GAR serves primarily as a \\emph{diagnostic probe}: it measures whether auxiliary attention training transfers to autoregressive generation, rather than being an end-to-end optimization objective whose increase directly improves downstream task performance (the correlation with instruction retention is weak; $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\\% decay; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,3.3\\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nDifferent transformer layers serve different roles: early layers capture local syntax while later layers integrate long-range context \\citep{gromov2024unreasonable}. A global intervention would uniformly scale all layers, but goal attention decay is layer-dependent: some layers contribute more to cross-turn information routing than others. ThreadGate therefore adds a \\emph{per-layer} gating mechanism to the residual stream, allowing the model to learn which layers to modulate. At each layer $l$, the residual update is scaled by a scalar gate:\n\\begin{equation}\n  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or feed-forward network, FFN). From the perspective that residual networks function as ensembles of shallow paths \\citep{veit2016residual}, per-layer gating selectively modulates individual paths. This resembles ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training. ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \\emph{content-conditioned}, a function of the hidden state rather than a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \\emph{turn-conditioned}, incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\\sigma(1.5) \\approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Gate network.} A fixed scalar per layer (as in ReZero) cannot adapt to varying inputs; the gate must be \\emph{content-conditioned} so that different conversations or turns can receive different modulation. Adding \\emph{turn conditioning} allows the gate to distinguish early turns (where GAR is naturally high) from late turns (where intervention is most needed). Each gate $g_l$ is thus produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:\n\\begin{align}\\label{eq:gate}\n  g_l = \\sigma\\bigl(W_2\\,\\text{ReLU}(&W_1\\,[\\text{proj}(x_l);\\;\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \\notag\\\\\n  &+ b_1) + b_2\\bigr),\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\text{proj}(\\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\\!=\\!28$), this adds ${\\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\\% of 7B).\n\n\\paragraph{Turn embedding.} The embedding $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ encodes turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables, allowing gates to distinguish conversation positions. The \\textbf{Uniform Gate} ablation replaces $\\text{emb}(\\cdot)$ with a learnable constant, removing turn awareness while retaining a learnable content-conditioned MLP gate.\n\n\\paragraph{Initialization.} Gate biases are set to 1.5 (${\\sim}$82\\% pass-through), contrasting ReZero's zero initialization that would suppress pretrained capabilities. Gate parameters receive a $10\\times$ learning rate multiplier.\n\n\\subsection{Training Objective}\\label{sec:loss}\n\nNext-token prediction alone provides no gradient signal for attention allocation; a model can achieve low perplexity while distributing attention arbitrarily. The auxiliary GAR loss provides explicit pressure to maintain goal-token attention, while a diversity regularizer prevents gate collapse. The training objective combines three terms:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\,\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\,\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}.\n\\end{equation}\n$\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. We use mean squared error (MSE) rather than cross-entropy for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ because GAR is a continuous ratio, not a probability, and MSE provides \\emph{bidirectional} pressure, penalizing both under-attention (GAR\\,$<$\\,$\\tau$) and over-attention (GAR\\,$>$\\,$\\tau$). This symmetry is critical: a unidirectional maximize objective drives gates toward identity (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}). The GAR loss is:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} = \\text{MSE}(\\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}, \\tau),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tau = 0.5$ targets allocating half of the last token's attention capacity to goal tokens. GAR is measured at the last token of each assistant turn (GAR-ours, \\S\\ref{sec:background}). Without explicit diversity pressure, all gates converge to the same value (typically near 1.0), eliminating the layer-specific modulation that is the design intent. The gate diversity loss prevents this collapse:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L}_\\text{div} = \\text{ReLU}(\\sigma_\\text{min} - \\text{std}(\\mathbf{g})),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{g} = [g_1, \\ldots, g_L]$ and $\\sigma_\\text{min} = 0.05$ is a minimum standard deviation threshold. Both $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Loss design evolution.} The MSE formulation was selected from seven iterations spanning four loss families (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}): cumulative (v1--v2) and maximize (v3--v6) objectives either starve gate gradients at equilibrium or drive gates toward identity. Only MSE provides bidirectional pressure, penalizing both over- and under-attention, preventing identity collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Controlled Comparison Design}\\label{sec:comparison-design}\n\nWe design a five-way comparison where each adjacent pair isolates one factor: \\textbf{ThreadGate (TG) vs.\\ Uniform Gate (UG)} (turn conditioning), \\textbf{TG vs.\\ LoRA-only} (gating mechanism), \\textbf{ThreadGate vs.\\ +PCGrad} (gradient conflict via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}), and \\textbf{Fine-tuned vs.\\ Base} (LoRA adaptation). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause (9\\% PPL cost at $\\beta$\\,=\\,3; phase transition at $\\beta \\in [2, 3]$).}\n  \\label{fig:steering-recovery}\n\\end{figure}\n",
