    "oldString": "$\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. We use mean squared error (MSE) rather than cross-entropy for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ because GAR is a continuous ratio, not a probability, and MSE provides \\emph{bidirectional} pressure, penalizing both under-attention (GAR\\,$<$\\,$\\tau$) and over-attention (GAR\\,$>$\\,$\\tau$). This symmetry is critical: a unidirectional maximize objective drives gates toward identity (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}). The GAR loss is:",
    "newString": "$\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. We use MSE rather than cross-entropy for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ because GAR is a continuous ratio and MSE provides \\emph{bidirectional} pressure---penalizing both under- and over-attention, preventing identity collapse (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}):",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Preliminaries}\\label{sec:background}\n\n\\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}\nStandard metrics such as perplexity and attention entropy capture generation quality but not \\emph{what} the model attends to. In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \\emph{goal tokens} $\\mathcal{G}$. In the standard transformer attention mechanism \\citep{vaswani2017attention}, \\citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\\tau$ as\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar}\n  \\text{GAR}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H \\cdot |\\mathcal{R}_\\tau|} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{i \\in \\mathcal{R}_\\tau} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathcal{R}_\\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\\ell$, head $h$.\n\n\\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}\n\\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. However, averaging dilutes the signal with intermediate positions that may legitimately attend to local context rather than the system prompt. The last token of each assistant turn is where the model aggregates information for the next continuation---attention at this position most directly reflects the model's awareness of task specifications. \\textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement accordingly:\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:gar-ours}\n  \\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}(\\tau) = \\frac{1}{L \\cdot H} \\sum_{\\ell=1}^{L} \\sum_{h=1}^{H} \\sum_{j \\in \\mathcal{G}} A_{t_\\tau,\\, j}^{(\\ell,h)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $t_\\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). Throughout this work, GAR serves primarily as a \\emph{diagnostic probe}: it measures whether auxiliary attention training transfers to autoregressive generation, rather than being an end-to-end optimization objective whose increase directly improves downstream task performance (the correlation with instruction retention is weak; $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\\% decay; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,3.3\\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nDifferent transformer layers serve different roles: early layers capture local syntax while later layers integrate long-range context \\citep{gromov2024unreasonable}. A global intervention would uniformly scale all layers, but goal attention decay is layer-dependent: some layers contribute more to cross-turn information routing than others. ThreadGate therefore adds a \\emph{per-layer} gating mechanism to the residual stream, allowing the model to learn which layers to modulate. At each layer $l$, the residual update is scaled by a scalar gate:\n\\begin{equation}\n  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or feed-forward network, FFN). From the perspective that residual networks function as ensembles of shallow paths \\citep{veit2016residual}, per-layer gating selectively modulates individual paths. This resembles ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training. ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \\emph{content-conditioned}, a function of the hidden state rather than a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \\emph{turn-conditioned}, incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\\sigma(1.5) \\approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Gate network.} Each gate $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:\n\\begin{align}\\label{eq:gate}\n  g_l = \\sigma\\bigl(W_2\\,\\text{ReLU}(&W_1\\,[\\text{proj}(x_l);\\;\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \\notag\\\\\n  &+ b_1) + b_2\\bigr),\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\text{proj}(\\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\\!=\\!28$), this adds ${\\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\\% of 7B).\n\n\\paragraph{Turn embedding.} The embedding $\\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ encodes turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables, allowing gates to distinguish conversation positions. The \\textbf{Uniform Gate} ablation replaces $\\text{emb}(\\cdot)$ with a learnable constant, removing turn awareness while retaining a learnable content-conditioned MLP gate.\n\n\\paragraph{Initialization.} Gate biases are set to 1.5 (${\\sim}$82\\% pass-through), contrasting ReZero's zero initialization that would suppress pretrained capabilities. Gate parameters receive a $10\\times$ learning rate multiplier.\n\n\\subsection{Training Objective}\\label{sec:loss}\n\nNext-token prediction alone provides no gradient signal for attention allocation; a model can achieve low perplexity while distributing attention arbitrarily. The auxiliary GAR loss provides explicit pressure to maintain goal-token attention, while a diversity regularizer prevents gate collapse. The training objective combines three terms:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\,\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\,\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}.\n\\end{equation}\n$\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. We use mean squared error (MSE) rather than cross-entropy for $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ because GAR is a continuous ratio, not a probability, and MSE provides \\emph{bidirectional} pressure, penalizing both under-attention (GAR\\,$<$\\,$\\tau$) and over-attention (GAR\\,$>$\\,$\\tau$). This symmetry is critical: a unidirectional maximize objective drives gates toward identity (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}). The GAR loss is:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} = \\text{MSE}(\\text{GAR\\text{-}ours}, \\tau),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tau = 0.5$ targets allocating half of the last token's attention capacity to goal tokens. GAR is measured at the last token of each assistant turn (GAR-ours, \\S\\ref{sec:background}). Without explicit diversity pressure, all gates converge to the same value (typically near 1.0), eliminating the layer-specific modulation that is the design intent. The gate diversity loss prevents this collapse:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathcal{L}_\\text{div} = \\text{ReLU}(\\sigma_\\text{min} - \\text{std}(\\mathbf{g})),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{g} = [g_1, \\ldots, g_L]$ and $\\sigma_\\text{min} = 0.05$ is a minimum standard deviation threshold. Both $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Loss design evolution.} The MSE formulation was selected from seven iterations spanning four loss families (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}): cumulative (v1--v2) and maximize (v3--v6) objectives either starve gate gradients at equilibrium or drive gates toward identity. Only MSE provides bidirectional pressure, penalizing both over- and under-attention, preventing identity collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Controlled Comparison Design}\\label{sec:comparison-design}\n\nWe design a five-way comparison where each adjacent pair isolates one factor: \\textbf{ThreadGate (TG) vs.\\ Uniform Gate (UG)} (turn conditioning), \\textbf{TG vs.\\ LoRA-only} (gating mechanism), \\textbf{ThreadGate vs.\\ +PCGrad} (gradient conflict via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}), and \\textbf{Fine-tuned vs.\\ Base} (LoRA adaptation). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as the proximal cause (9\\% PPL cost at $\\beta$\\,=\\,3; phase transition at $\\beta \\in [2, 3]$).}\n  \\label{fig:steering-recovery}\n\\end{figure}\n",

This symmetry is critical: a unidirectional maximize objective drives gates toward identity (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-loss}). The GAR loss is:",

    "oldString": "The five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain; each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.",
    "newString": "Each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis via causal exclusion: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}~turn conditioning, \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad}~gradient conflict, leaving exposure bias as the surviving explanation (\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift}). \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity; \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms cross-architecture generalization.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain; each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$); e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, a convergence pattern reminiscent of attention collapse in deep transformers \\citep{dong2021attention}, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane, should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing gradient conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ magnitude dominance renders gradient conflict inconsequential. Cross-model analysis confirms this pattern: Llama-3.1-8B gradients are near-orthogonal (cos\\,=\\,+0.005) with 25.7$\\times$ NTP dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:gradient-crossmodel}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_multiseed.pdf}\n  \\caption{Multi-seed AR GAR under scheduled sampling ($\\beta$\\,=\\,0 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, three seeds). Steering nearly eliminates seed variance (std: 0.047$\\to$0.001).}\n  \\label{fig:ss-multiseed}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, confirming that the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants. Pilot gaps (20--34$\\times$) exceed final $N$\\,=\\,50 estimates (8--17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}) due to smaller sample size.}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20, 4 turns). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) / 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (coefficient of variation, CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%); we report medians: 0.018 (TG and LoRA-only), 0.009 (Base), yielding gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}). Gate profiles differ qualitatively (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793) while Llama dips at late layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), an architecture-dependent pattern, but convergence toward identity is universal (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\\times$ on Qwen, 27$\\times$ on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as architecture-independent.\n\n\\enlargethispage{2\\baselineskip}\nScheduled sampling confirms cross-model generalization: Llama SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$12$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (TF GAR 0.477, AR GAR 0.166), weaker than Qwen's ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3). The phase transition is architecture-dependent: $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 triggers recovery on Qwen (0.556) but not Llama (0.173); Llama requires $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 (GAR 0.791, exceeding Qwen SS 0.717), suggesting deeper architectures need higher steering thresholds but ultimately provide more surface for attention reweighting (full results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}). Cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B confirms the same pattern: 79.8\\% GAR decay and $\\beta$-steering recovery to 0.715 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-1.5b}).",
